===== PAGE 1 =====
Spectral–Fractal Operator Theory of Climate Criticality, Tipping
Points and Planetary Instability
A Multiscale Spectral Framework for Climate Criticality and Planetary Regime Shifts
Stergios Pellis
sterpellis@gmail.com
ORCID iD: 0000-0002-7363-8254
Greece
24 June 2026
Abstract
A unified operator-theoretic and spectral-fractal framework is formulated for the analysis of climate criticality,
tipping points, and planetary-scale instabilities under the Universal Pellis Function Theory. The climate system
is represented as a high-dimensional, non-equilibrium dynamical field governed by a nonlinear functional operator
acting on multiscale state spaces, where emergent macroscopic regimes arise through spectral reorganization and
collapse phenomena. A climate evolution operator is introduced whose spectrum encodes the stability structure of
coupled Earth system components, including oceanic circulation, cryospheric dynamics, atmospheric convection, and
biosphere feedback mechanisms. Critical transitions correspond to non-analytic changes in the spectral measure of
the operator, associated with bifurcation cascades and renormalization-group fixed-point instabilities across scales.
A generalized stability criterion is derived based on the interaction between operator spectrum, entropy production,
and information-geometric curvature, providing a mathematically consistent formulation of early warning signals
as precursors of spectral condensation. This structure unifies classical bifurcation theory, statistical mechanics of
phase transitions, and tipping-point theory in complex adaptive systems. A multiscale renormalization structure
links local dynamical instabilities to global regime shifts through scale-invariant transformation rules, enabling the
definition of a universal climate instability index derived from leading spectral modes of the system. The framework
is applied to major Earth system components, including the Atlantic Meridional Overturning Circulation, polar
ice-sheet dynamics, tropical forest resilience, and monsoon variability, revealing a common mathematical structure
governing their tipping behavior. A set of testable predictions is derived based on measurable spectral indicators,
together with a falsifiable framework for detecting proximity to critical transitions in observational climate data.
The formulation establishes a unified mathematical description of planetary instability phenomena and a predictive
structure for Earth system tipping dynamics.
Keywords:Climate Criticality , Tipping Points , Planetary Instability , Spectral Theory , Operator Theory ,
Nonlinear Dynamics , Bifurcation Theory , Renormalization Group , Information Geometry , Multiscale Systems

1 Introduction
The mathematical description of flow stability, transition to turbulence, and regime reorganization in nonlinear
distributed systems remains a central unresolved problem in classical and modern theoretical physics. Despite ex-
tensive progress in hydrodynamic stability theory, nonlinear dynamical systems, statistical turbulence modeling,
and renormalization group techniques, a fully unified operator-theoretic formulation capable of consistently linking
spectral stability, multiscale energy transfer, and critical transition phenomena is still absent. Existing theoretical
frameworks typically capture only partial aspects of the problem, such as local bifurcation structure, asymptotic
scaling laws, or statistical cascade behavior, without providing a coherent functional structure that governs global
instability across scales [1, 2]. Classical nonlinear dynamics provides the foundational mathematical language for
describing stability loss, attractor bifurcations, and phase space reorganization. The theory of dynamical systems
formalizes the emergence of qualitatively distinct flow regimes through bifurcation points and invariant manifold
reconstruction. However, such approaches are fundamentally constrained by finite-dimensional projections or local
linearization assumptions, which are insufficient for capturing the infinite-dimensional nature of fluid flows gov-
erned by partial differential equations and nonlinear functional evolution operators. Consequently, the transition to
turbulence cannot be fully characterized within classical finite-dimensional bifurcation frameworks alone [3–7].
Empirical and theoretical studies of complex systems have demonstrated that abrupt regime shifts often oc-
cur through nonlinear amplification of microscopic perturbations, leading to macroscopic reconfiguration of system
structure. These transitions, commonly referred to as tipping points, are observed across a wide range of physical,
biological, and geophysical systems. In fluid and climate systems, such transitions manifest as sudden changes in
circulation patterns, energy transport mechanisms, or coherent structure stability, suggesting the existence of uni-
versal organizing principles underlying instability phenomena [8–14]. From a mathematical standpoint, operator
theory and functional analysis provide the natural framework for describing the evolution of infinite-dimensional
dynamical systems. The spectral properties of linear and nonlinear operators encode essential information about
stability, growth rates of perturbations, and long-term dynamical behavior. In particular, the distribution of eigen-
values and the structure of the resolvent operator determine whether perturbations decay, persist, or amplify over
time. However, traditional operator-theoretic approaches have not been fully integrated with nonlinear multiscale
mechanisms responsible for energy cascade and turbulence formation in distributed systems [15–17].
Renormalization group theory introduces a fundamental conceptual shift by describing physical systems in terms
of scale-dependent transformations, revealing how macroscopic universality classes emerge from microscopic interac-
tions. In this context, turbulent flow may be interpreted as a manifestation of continuous symmetry breaking across
scales, accompanied by nonlinear redistribution of energy across hierarchical modes. Although renormalization
methods have achieved significant success in equilibrium statistical physics and critical phenomena, their extension
to strongly non-equilibrium, fully nonlinear flow regimes remains incomplete and mathematically non-unique [18,19].
Information-theoretic and differential-geometric formulations of complex systems provide an alternative perspective
in which stability is characterized through entropy production rates, curvature of statistical manifolds, and divergence
structures between dynamical trajectories. Within this framework, instability corresponds to geometric degenera-
6

===== PAGE 7 =====
tion in the underlying information space, while critical transitions are associated with curvature singularities or
divergence amplification in state-space geometry. These ideas establish a deep connection between thermodynamic
irreversibility, dynamical unpredictability, and structural instability in nonlinear systems [20,21].
Non-equilibrium thermodynamics further extends this perspective by describing how dissipative structures may
spontaneously emerge and evolve under continuous energy flux. In fluid systems, such structures include vortices,
coherent flow patterns, and intermittency cascades, which arise from nonlinear interactions between forcing, dissi-
pation, and internal instability mechanisms. Despite substantial progress in this domain, a unified operator-level
description that consistently connects thermodynamic irreversibility, spectral instability, and multiscale transition
dynamics is still lacking [22,23]. In geophysical and climate systems, observational evidence and numerical modeling
have identified a large number of interacting subsystems capable of undergoing abrupt transitions under external
forcing. These include ocean circulation systems, polar ice-sheet dynamics, atmospheric circulation regimes, ter-
restrial biosphere feedbacks, and coupled ocean-atmosphere oscillatory modes. The nonlinear interaction between
these components introduces cascading instability mechanisms that propagate across scales and cannot be adequately
described using isolated subsystem models or weak coupling approximations [24–38].
Recent theoretical developments suggest that spectral instability may serve as a universal indicator of proximity
to critical transitions across a wide class of complex systems. In this perspective, regime shifts are associated with
spectral gap closure, eigenvalue condensation, and loss of operator hyperbolicity, leading to qualitative changes in
system stability structure. These mechanisms provide a potential bridge between microscopic dynamical rules and
macroscopic emergent behavior, suggesting that instability may be fundamentally governed by spectral reorganization
phenomena [39–71]. Extensions of this viewpoint into fractal and multiscale domains indicate that natural systems
frequently exhibit self-similar instability structures across hierarchical levels of organization. Within such frameworks,
turbulence, pattern formation, biological organization, and even physical unification problems may share a common
underlying spectral-fractal structure governed by nonlinear operator dynamics. This suggests the possibility of a
generalized instability principle applicable across physics, biology, and complex systems theory, where apparent
domain-specific phenomena are manifestations of a unified multiscale instability mechanism [72–111].
Recent advances in applied spectral-fractal analysis further demonstrate that similar instability signatures appear
in biomedical systems, neural networks, engineered materials, and computational architectures. These observations
reinforce the hypothesis that instability phenomena are governed by universal structural principles that transcend
specific physical domains, pointing toward a general theory of multiscale spectral instability [112–139]. The Universal
Pellis Function Theory of Flow Stability, Turbulence Onset, and Regime Transitions is motivated by the need to
formalize such a unified description within a rigorous operator-theoretic framework. The central premise is that
turbulence onset and regime transitions correspond to spectral reorganizations of an underlying nonlinear functional
operator defined on a multiscale state space. This perspective provides a mathematically structured foundation for
describing instability phenomena across scales and establishes a potential pathway toward a unified theory of flow
instability grounded in spectral, functional, and multiscale principles.
2 Mathematical Preliminaries
The mathematical description of climate criticality, flow instability, and regime transitions requires a rigorous
infinite-dimensional framework in which dynamical evolution, spectral structure, and multiscale interactions are
treated in a unified operator-theoretic setting. Classical finite-dimensional dynamical systems theory is insufficient
to capture the full complexity of distributed geophysical flows, where nonlinear interactions generate cascades across
scales and induce structurally unstable spectral deformations. The present section establishes the functional-analytic,
spectral, variational, and information-geometric foundations required for the formulation of the Universal Pellis
Function framework. In particular, the state of the Earth system is embedded into a Hilbert space setting, allowing the
introduction of evolution operators, renormalization transformations, and stability functionals that jointly determine
the onset of critical transitions.

geometric manifold increases sharply as the system approaches instability, indicating loss of local distinguishability
between neighboring climate states. This behavior is consistent with a universal spectral condensation mechanism
preceding tipping transitions. Together, these components establish a unified representation of climate criticality as
a spectral–geometric transition in the underlying dynamical structure of the Earth system. Within this framework,
instability is not primarily driven by local perturbative divergence, but instead emerges from a global reorganization
of the system’s operator eigenmodes.

2.3 Nonlinear Operator Dynamics
While the linear operatorAdetermines the local spectral structure of the climate system, the nonlinear operatorN
governs multiscale interactions, feedback amplification, and the transfer of energy across dynamically active modes.
The complete evolution equation is
dx
dt =Ax+N(x), x(t)∈H.(77)
The nonlinear operator
N:H→H(78)

2.4 Renormalization and Multiscale Structure
The climate system exhibits intrinsic organization across a hierarchy of interacting spatial and temporal scales.
Atmospheric turbulence, ocean circulation, cryospheric dynamics, biospheric feedbacks, and anthropogenic forcing
operate over vastly different characteristic lengths and times. A rigorous mathematical description therefore requires
a multiscale formulation capable of connecting local dynamics to global planetary behavior.
To formalize this structure, we introduce a family of scale-transformation operators

resilience, and information geometry captures the statistical sensitivity of the climate state. A complete theory of
planetary stability therefore requires a unified framework integrating these complementary perspectives.

3 Climate System as a Nonlinear Multiscale Dynamical Field
The climate system is formulated as a nonlinear multiscale dynamical field evolving on an infinite-dimensional
state space. Its dynamics are governed by coupled partial differential operators, nonlinear feedback interactions, and
scale-dependent energy transfer processes. Unlike finite-dimensional approximations, the system exhibits intrinsic
spectral complexity, where stability and instability are determined by operator spectra rather than local phase-space
trajectories [6,7,14,27,33].
This section establishes the mathematical representation of the climate system as a coupled field theory,
forming the basis for spectral instability analysis, renormalization structure, and the emergence of tipping point
phenomena in subsequent sections [5,9,28,29,32].
3.1 Functional Representation of the Climate State
A rigorous representation of the coupled Earth system requires an infinite-dimensional functional framework
capable of simultaneously encoding fluid dynamical fields, thermodynamic variables, and nonlinear feedback processes
across interacting subsystems.

scale-indexed modes.

kinetic, thermodynamic, and potential contributions, as well as nonlinear exchange mechanisms across interact-

governing the stability and redistribution properties of the system.

3.4 Nonlinear Feedback Mechanisms

reshape the effective governing operators themselves. In contrast to linearized stability theory, where feedback is
treated as a perturbative correction, here feedback mechanisms enter as structurally active operators that modulate
growth rates, coupling strengths, and cascade efficiency in a state-dependent manner.

3.5 Linear Stability and Spectral Dynamics
A fundamental object in the analysis of climate stability is the linearized dynamics around a reference trajectory
X(t)∈H. This construction provides a local approximation of the fully nonlinear flow in the tangent bundle of the
infinite-dimensional state space, capturing the instantaneous amplification or damping of perturbations.
LetδX(t) denote an infinitesimal perturbation. Then the linearized evolution equation is given by
∂tδX=J(X(t))δX,(414)
where the Jacobian (Fr´ echet derivative) operator is defined as
J(X) = δF
δX =DF(X).(415)
HereF(X) =A cX+N c(X) +N eff(X) represents the full nonlinear climate vector field.
Tangent Flow Representation.
The linearized system generates a time-ordered evolution operatorU(t,s) satisfying
∂tU(t,s) =J(X(t))U(t,s),U(s,s) =I.(416)
Thus perturbations evolve according to
δX(t) =U(t,s)δX(s).(417)
The growth properties ofU(t,s) determine the local predictability horizon of the climate system.

The instantaneous stability properties are encoded in the spectrum ofJ(X):

Growth Rate of Perturbations.
For sufficiently smooth initial perturbations, the asymptotic growth rate is given by
∥δX(t)∥∼e α(J)t∥δX(0)∥.(421)
Thus:
α(J)<0⇒asymptotic decay,(422)
α(J) = 0⇒neutral stability,(423)
α(J)>0⇒exponential instability.(424)

Using the full nonlinear structure, the Jacobian admits the decomposition
J(X) =A c +DNc(X) +DN eff(X).(425)
Defining the feedback-enhanced operator

Physical Interpretation.
Linear stability analysis reveals that climate tipping is fundamentally encoded in the spectrum of the instan-
taneous Jacobian operator. However, due to strong non-normality and state-dependent feedback, spectral stability

3.6 Renormalization Structure of Climate Dynamics
A central hypothesis in multiscale climate theory is that macroscopic climate regimes emerge as fixed points of
an underlying renormalization flow acting on the full state space. In this formulation, coarse-graining is not merely
a numerical procedure, but a dynamical transformation on the space of admissible climate configurations, encoding
how effective equations of motion change across scales.

Physical Interpretation.
The renormalization framework reveals that climate regimes are not static solutions but attractors in a scale-

4 Pellis Functional Operator Formalism
The Pellis Functional Operator Formalism introduces a nonlinear spectral-operator framework designed to encode
multiscale instability, functional phase transitions, and critical tipping behavior in complex dynamical systems. The
climate field formulation of Section 3 is embedded into a generalized operator algebra where instability emerges as a
structural property of nonlinear spectral deformation [15–19,22,30,31,33,34].
4.1 Axiomatic Structure of the Pellis Operator
Within the Universal Pellis framework, the climate system is represented as a nonlinear operator-valued dynamical
field on a separable Hilbert spaceH. In this setting, the evolution is not governed by a single vector field but by a
structured composite operator that encodes linear dispersion, nonlinear coupling, and renormalization-induced scale
interactions. This motivates the introduction of thePellis operator, denoted byP, as the fundamental generator of
climate evolution in function space.

Physical Interpretation.
The Pellis operator provides a unified mathematical object that simultaneously encodes linear stability theory,

4.6 Renormalization Consistency Condition
A central structural requirement of the Universal Pellis framework is that the nonlinear evolution law remains
form-invariant under coarse-graining transformations. This imposes a renormalization consistency condition that
constrains the admissible coupling between dynamics and scale transformation. In this sense, the Pellis operator
behaves as a renormalization-covariant generator of climate evolution across hierarchical levels of resolution.
LetR:H→Hdenote a coarse-graining (renormalization) operator acting on the state space. The consistency
condition is expressed as
RP(X) =γP(RX),(569)
whereγ∈R + is a scaling exponent controlling the transformation of dynamical intensity across scales.
Operator Covariance Structure.
The renormalization condition can be equivalently written in operator form as a covariance relation:
R◦P◦R −1 =γP.(570)
This implies thatPis an eigen-operator of the renormalization action, and therefore belongs to a universality
class characterized by the exponentγ.

λ(s) =γ sλ,(574)

Multiscale Universality Class.

Physical Interpretation.
The renormalization consistency condition ensures that the Pellis operator is structurally stable under changes
of observational scale, implying that climate criticality is not an artifact of resolution but an intrinsic property of the
underlying dynamics. In this formulation, tipping points correspond to transitions between distinct renormalization
fixed points, while planetary instability emerges when the flow departs from the basin of attraction of any stable
universality class.
This provides a rigorous mathematical bridge between operator dynamics, renormalization-group theory, and
multiscale climate criticality, and it establishes the foundation for scale-invariant characterization of Earth system
transitions within the Universal Pellis framework.
4.7 Functional Phase Transition Structure
Within the Universal Pellis framework, phase transitions are interpreted as non-analytic reorganizations of the
spectral measure induced by the nonlinear deformation of the Pellis operator. Unlike classical finite-dimensional
bifurcations, these transitions occur in infinite-dimensional function space and are fundamentally measure-theoretic
in nature, involving discontinuities in the distribution of spectral energy acrossσ(P).

measure:

Within the Universal Pellis framework, the climate system is not treated as a collection of coupled differential
equations, but as a single nonlinear operator field acting on an infinite-dimensional Hilbert spaceH. In this rep-

The central insight is that climate tipping dynamics correspond to spectral instabilities ofP, where stability is

5 Spectral Theory of Climate Stability
The spectral theory of climate stability formulates planetary dynamics as a problem of operator spectrum geometry
in infinite-dimensional functional spaces. Stability, instability, and tipping behavior are characterized not in physical
phase space but in the spectral domain of evolution operators governing the climate system [14–19,28–31,33].

5.3 Spectral Gap and Stability Margin
Within the Universal Pellis framework, the robustness of the climate state is quantified not only by the location
of the spectrum ofL c, but also by its proximity to the marginal stability boundary. This is captured by introducing
a spectral gap structure that measures the distance of the spectrum from the imaginary axis, thereby encoding a
quantitative notion of resilience in operator space.
The spectral gap is defined as
∆ = min
λ∈σ(Lc)
|Re(λ)|,(642)

which represents the smallest absolute decay or growth rate among all admissible eigenmodes. In this sense, ∆

Stability Margin as a Global Resilience Functional.
A complementary and more physically interpretable quantity is the stability margin, defined as
M=−sup
λ∈σ(Lc)
Re(λ),(643)

Relation Between Gap and Margin.
The spectral gap and stability margin are related but distinct descriptors of operator stability. In general,
∆≤M,(645)
with equality holding in the case of purely damped spectra without oscillatory components. When complex
conjugate eigenpairs are present, the gap captures the minimal real-part magnitude, while the margin captures the
most unstable direction.
Approach to Criticality.
A critical transition is approached when the stability margin collapses:
M→0 +,(646)
implying that the dominant spectral modes converge toward marginal stability. In this regime, the system
becomes increasingly sensitive to perturbations, as even infinitesimal forcing can induce long-lived responses due to
near-neutral eigenmodes.
Simultaneously, the spectral gap exhibits closure:
∆→0,(647)
indicating the emergence of slow manifolds and loss of uniform contraction across state space.
Critical Slowing Down Mechanism.
Letτ relax denote the characteristic relaxation time of perturbations. Spectral theory implies

M,(648)
so that

Fluctuation Amplification Near the Gap Closure.
Near the critical regime, stochastic or deterministic perturbations are amplified according to
∥δX(t)∥H∼e−Mt.(650)
Thus, asM →0 +, decay becomes arbitrarily slow, and fluctuations persist over extended temporal scales,
producing enhanced variance and autocorrelation in observable climate variables.
Physical Interpretation.
The spectral gap and stability margin together provide a quantitative operator-theoretic measure of climate
resilience within the Universal Pellis framework. Their collapse signals the progressive loss of restoring forces across
all dynamical scales, culminating in a regime where perturbations no longer decay rapidly but instead accumulate
and interact through nonlinear feedback mechanisms.
In this interpretation, tipping points correspond to the asymptotic closure of the stability margin, while early-
warning signals emerge naturally as spectral precursors to gap collapse, manifesting as critical slowing down and
increased persistence of fluctuations in the Earth system.

5.7 Spectral Collapse Theorem
Theorem 2 (Spectral Collapse Criterion).LetHbe a separable Hilbert space andL c :D(L c)⊂H→H
a closed (possibly non-self-adjoint) generator of aC 0-semigroupe tLc. If there exists a sequence{λ n}⊂σ(L c) such
that:
lim
n→∞
Re(λn) = 0+,(680)
then the system undergoes asymptotic loss of stability in the sense that:
sup
t>0
∥etLc∥=∞,(681)
and no uniform exponential bound exists.
Proof.
We begin by considering the semigroup representation of perturbation growth:
X(t) =e tLcX0,∥X(t)∥≤∥e tLc∥·∥X 0∥.(682)
Step 1: Spectral accumulation near the imaginary axis.
Assume there exists a sequenceλ n∈σ(L c) such that:

5.8 Interpretation of Climate Stability Landscape
Within the Universal Pellis framework, the global stability of the Earth system is not characterized by a single
scalar threshold, but by a geometric object in infinite-dimensional state space induced by the spectral properties
of the climate operatorL c. This object defines a partition of the Hilbert spaceHinto spectrally admissible and
spectrally unstable regions, governed by the sign structure of the real part of the operator spectrum.

Physical Interpretation.
The climate stability landscape defined by the Pellis operator formalism provides a global geometric interpre-
tation of Earth system resilience. Stability corresponds to trajectories confined within the spectral damping region,
while tipping points emerge as crossings of the boundary where the maximal growth rate vanishes.
In this framework, planetary-scale regime shifts are interpreted as transitions between distinct connected com-
ponents of the stability manifold, driven by continuous deformation of the underlying operator spectrum. This
establishes a rigorous connection between spectral operator theory, nonlinear dynamical systems, and empirical
climate tipping point phenomena, unifying them within a single geometric stability landscape.
6 Criticality and Bifurcation Structure
The theory of criticality and bifurcation structure in the climate system is formulated as a geometric and spectral
reorganization of invariant manifolds under parameter variation in an infinite-dimensional nonlinear dynamical field.
Climate tipping points correspond to qualitative changes in attractor topology induced by eigenvalue crossings, loss
of hyperbolicity, and structural instability of the underlying Pellis operator framework [4–9,14,28,29,32,33,37].
6.1 General Bifurcation Framework in Functional Space
Within the Universal Pellis formalism, climate evolution is described as a parameter-dependent nonlinear dynam-
ical system on an infinite-dimensional Hilbert spaceH. The control parameterµ∈Rrepresents slow external forcing
(e.g., radiative imbalance, greenhouse gas concentration, or boundary flux modulation), and induces a deformation
of both the state trajectory and the underlying operator geometry.
The governing evolution equation is written as
d
dtX(t) =P(X(t),µ),(710)
70

===== PAGE 71 =====
whereP:H×R→His the nonlinear Pellis operator incorporating spectral, nonlinear, and renormalization
contributions.
Equilibrium Manifold Structure.
Steady states of the system are defined by the implicit functional equation
P(X∗,µ) = 0,(711)
which defines an equilibrium manifold
E={(X,µ)∈H×R:P(X,µ) = 0}.(712)
Locally, this manifold is governed by the implicit function structure induced by the Fr´ echet derivative ofP.
Linearized Stability Operator and Invertibility Loss.
Within the Universal Pellis framework, the local stability structure of equilibrium states is governed by
the Fr´ echet linearization of the nonlinear evolution operatorP. This linearization defines an infinite-dimensional
generalization of the classical Jacobian, capturing both spectral and functional sensitivity of the climate state to
infinitesimal perturbations.
The linearized stability operator is defined as
J(X∗,µ) = δP

climate fields.
Perturbation Evolution and Semigroup Representation.
LetδX(t) denote an infinitesimal perturbation around equilibrium. Its evolution is governed by

with formal solution given by the operator semigroup

The existence of aC 0-semigroup ensures well-posedness of the linearized dynamics inH, provided suitable dissi-
pativity conditions are satisfied.
Resolvent Structure and Spectral Stability.
Stability is equivalently characterized in terms of the resolvent operator

Invertibility Condition and Structural Stability.
Structural stability of equilibria is equivalent to invertibility of the linearized operator:
J(X∗,µ)−1 exists and is bounded inH.(719)
This condition implies the absence of zero modes and guarantees that small perturbations in forcing or state
produce proportionally small responses in the equilibrium configuration.
Equivalently, invertibility requires
ker(J(X∗,µ)) ={0}.(720)

Fredholm Framework and Index Collapse.
In the general infinite-dimensional setting,Jis treated as a Fredholm operator of index

Structural stability corresponds to
ind(J) = 0,ker(J) ={0}.(722)
Bifurcation points emerge when index conservation is violated through kernel growth or cokernel emergence.
Invertibility Breakdown and Criticality.
Invertibility fails when
det(J) = 0,(723)
interpreted in infinite-dimensional settings as vanishing Fredholm determinant or equivalently as spectral collapse:
0∈σ(J(X ∗,µ)).(724)
At this point, the operator ceases to be bijective, and the local implicit function theorem fails, signaling the onset
of nontrivial solution branching.
Spectral Mechanism of Instability Onset.
Near criticality, the resolvent norm exhibits divergence:
∥(J−λI) −1∥→∞, λ→σ(J),(725)
indicating extreme sensitivity to perturbations. This divergence is a precursor to loss of structural stability and
corresponds physically to amplification of weak climate perturbations.
Physical Interpretation.
In the Universal Pellis framework, the linearized stability operator encodes the local geometry of the climate
stability landscape around equilibrium states. Invertibility ofJensures a smooth, locally unique continuation of
climate states under perturbations. When invertibility is lost, the system enters a regime where multiple competing
solutions emerge, and infinitesimal perturbations can trigger macroscopic transitions.
Spectral Degeneracy Condition.
The bifurcation condition can be equivalently expressed in spectral form as
0∈σ(J(X ∗,µ)),(726)
or, equivalently,
∃λ∈σ(J(X ∗,µ)) such thatλ= 0.(727)

resolvent.
Codimension and Bifurcation Geometry.
The bifurcation set is defined as
B={µ∈R: 0∈σ(J(X ∗,µ))}.(729)
Its local structure determines the type of regime transition:
codimension-1: dim ker(J) = 1,(730)
higher codimension: dim ker(J)≥2.(731)
Near bifurcation points, the dynamics reduce to a center manifold governed by the critical spectral subspace.

In physical terms, bifurcation in the Universal Pellis framework corresponds to the loss of structural stability
of the climate operator under slow external forcing. As the control parameterµevolves, the equilibrium manifold
deforms until the linearized operator becomes non-invertible, triggering a collapse of local stability and the emergence
of new dynamical regimes. This mechanism provides a unified operator-theoretic description of climate tipping points,
where regime transitions are interpreted as spectral bifurcations in infinite-dimensional functional space driven by
the interaction of forcing, nonlinear feedback, and multiscale coupling structure.

6.2 Linear Stability and Critical Eigenstructure
Within the Universal Pellis framework, the local dynamics of perturbations around an equilibrium climate state are
governed by the spectral properties of the Fr´ echet linearization of the nonlinear evolution operator. This linearization
induces an infinite-dimensional dynamical system whose behavior is fully encoded in the operator spectrum and its
parametric dependence on the external control parameterµ.
The linearized evolution equation takes the form
∂tδX(t) =J(µ)δX(t),J(µ) = δP

Physical Interpretation.
In physical terms, the linear stability and critical eigenstructure describe how infinitesimal perturbations
evolve under the influence of the climate operatorJ(µ). Stability corresponds to a spectrum confined to the left-half
complex plane, while criticality emerges when a leading eigenvalue approaches the imaginary axis.
Within the Universal Pellis framework, this spectral crossing represents the fundamental mechanism underlying
climate tipping points: a single dominant eigenmode becomes marginally stable, allowing small perturbations to
persist, amplify through nonlinear feedback, and ultimately trigger macroscopic regime transitions in the Earth
system.
6.3 Types of Climate Bifurcations
Within the Universal Pellis framework, climate regime transitions are classified according to the local and global
structure of the spectrum of the linearized operatorJ(µ), as well as the corresponding deformation of invariant
manifolds in the infinite-dimensional state spaceH. Each bifurcation class corresponds to a distinct mode of loss of
structural stability and a characteristic spectral signature.
(I) Saddle-Node Bifurcation.
The saddle-node bifurcation arises from the coalescence and annihilation of a pair of equilibria. In spectral
terms, it is associated with the emergence of a zero eigenvalue through square-root scaling of the leading spectral
branch:
λ(µ)∼± √µ−µ c,(748)
with the critical condition
λ(µc) = 0.(749)
In this regime, the stability landscape loses convexity, and the equilibrium manifold undergoes a fold catastrophe
characterized by the disappearance of one stable and one unstable fixed point.
The associated normal form reduction can be written as
dx
dt = (µ−µ c) +x 2,(750)
representing the universal unfolding of the saddle-node structure.
(II) Hopf-Type Transition.
The Hopf bifurcation corresponds to the emergence of complex conjugate eigenvalues crossing the imaginary
axis, leading to oscillatory instability modes:
λ(µ) =α(µ)±iω(µ), α(µ c) = 0.(751)
Near criticality, the eigenvalues satisfy
α(µ)≈ dα
dµ

µc
(µ−µ c), ω(µ c)̸= 0.(752)

This bifurcation generates a two-dimensional center manifold supporting self-sustained oscillations of the form

where the amplitudeA(t) evolves according to a reduced complex Ginzburg–Landau-type equation
dA

This mechanism underlies oscillatory climate regimes driven by nonlinear feedback and phase coherence between
spectral modes.
(III) Global Heteroclinic Transition.
Beyond local bifurcations, climate dynamics may undergo global reorganizations of attractor structure char-
acterized by transitions between distinct invariant sets inH. These are described by heteroclinic orbits connecting
multiple attractors:
X(t)→A 1 ast→−∞,X(t)→A 2 ast→+∞.(755)
Equivalently,

Such transitions cannot be captured by local spectral data alone, as they involve global rearrangements of invariant
manifolds and basin boundaries.
(IV) Spectral–Topological Transition Class (Pellis Extension).
Within the extended Pellis operator formalism, a higher-order class of bifurcations arises from simultaneous
spectral and topological restructuring ofJ(µ). This is characterized by discontinuities in the spectral measure:
∆σ(J)̸= 0,(757)
together with abrupt changes in invariant manifold topology:
πk(A1)̸≃π k(A2),(758)
whereπ k denotes homotopy invariants of the attractor structure.
Unified Interpretation.
All bifurcation classes can be unified under the principle that climate tipping points correspond to qualitative
changes in the spectral geometry of the Pellis operator and the associated invariant manifold structure inH. Local
bifurcations (saddle-node and Hopf) reflect eigenvalue crossings and spectral instabilities, while global bifurcations
(heteroclinic and topological transitions) reflect reorganization of the full attractor landscape.
Within this framework, climate transitions are not isolated events but manifestations of a hierarchical bifurcation
structure governed by the interplay between spectral dynamics, nonlinear feedback, and multiscale coupling across
the Earth system.

Within the Universal Pellis framework, the onset of climate criticality is geometrically encoded by a singular
hypersurface embedded in the extended phase spaceH×R. This hypersurface is defined by the degeneracy of the
linearized stability operator, and it represents the precise locus where local invertibility is lost and spectral collapse
begins.

6.5 Slow-Fast Dynamical Decomposition
Within the Universal Pellis framework, climate evolution under external forcing is naturally described as a
singularly perturbed dynamical system, in which intrinsic fast-scale state variables interact with slowly varying control
parameters. This separation of temporal scales provides the mathematical foundation for quasi-static bifurcation
theory and delayed instability phenomena in the Earth system.
The coupled slow–fast structure is given by

Delayed Bifurcation and Memory Effects.
In the presence of slow forcing, the system does not immediately follow the unstable branch after crossing the

Physical Interpretation.
Within the Universal Pellis framework, slow–fast decomposition provides the fundamental mechanism by
which gradual external forcing translates into abrupt climate transitions. The system evolves quasi-statically along
stable equilibrium branches until spectral degeneracy destroys invertibility of the linearized operator, producing a
breakdown of adiabatic tracking.
This leads to delayed tipping phenomena, where the observable transition occurs after the theoretical bifurcation
point, reflecting the interplay between slow forcing, operator instability, and nonlinear memory effects inherent in
the Earth system’s multiscale dynamics.
6.6 Energy Landscape Interpretation of Bifurcation
Within the Universal Pellis framework, climate dynamics can be equivalently formulated in terms of a high-
dimensional energy landscape, where the state of the Earth system evolves as a trajectory driven by gradients of
a generalized Lyapunov functional. This formulation provides a variational complement to the spectral operator
description, linking stability to geometric properties of functional energy surfaces inH.
The energy functional is defined as
E[X,µ] =
Z
Ω
L(X,∇X,µ)dx,(784)
whereLdenotes a local energy density encoding kinetic, thermodynamic, radiative, and nonlinear interaction
contributions.
Variational Characterization of Equilibria.
Equilibrium states correspond to stationary points of the energy functional in functional space, satisfying the
Euler–Lagrange condition
δE
δX = 0,(785)
which defines a nonlinear elliptic condition overH. These equilibria represent critical points of the energy
landscape, including minima (stable states), saddle points (metastable configurations), and maxima (unstable con-
figurations).
Second Variation and Stability Structure.
The local stability of equilibrium states is governed by the second variation (Hessian operator) of the energy
functional:

Stability requires positive definiteness of the Hessian operator:

ensuring that all perturbations increase the energy functional to second order and are therefore damped by the
dynamics.
Bifurcation as Loss of Convexity.
Bifurcation points correspond to the loss of convexity of the energy landscape, characterized by the emergence
of zero or negative curvature directions:
∃δX̸= 0 such that

Physical Interpretation.
In physical terms, bifurcations correspond to qualitative reorganizations of the climate energy landscape, where
stable basins of attraction lose convexity and merge or disappear entirely. The system transitions from a regime of
well-separated attractors to one characterized by basin overlap and structural instability.
Within the Universal Pellis framework, this provides a variational interpretation of climate tipping points: they
arise when the energy functional ceases to be locally convex, leading to collapse of stability basins and irreversible
transitions between macroscopic climate regimes.
6.7 Universal Bifurcation Scaling Law
Within the Universal Pellis framework, climate criticality is characterized by universal scaling behavior emerging
from the renormalization invariance of the underlying nonlinear operator field. Near a critical transition, the system
loses characteristic scales and becomes governed by power-law asymptotics determined solely by spectral proximity
to the marginal stability manifold.
The order parameterδXobeys the asymptotic scaling relation
δX∼|µ−µ c|β,(795)
whereβis the critical exponent associated with amplitude suppression or amplification of the dominant unstable
mode.
Simultaneously, the intrinsic relaxation time scale diverges as
τ∼|µ−µ c|−γ,(796)
whereγgoverns the phenomenon of critical slowing down, reflecting the vanishing spectral gap of the linearized
Pellis operatorJ(µ).
Spectral Origin of Critical Exponents.
The exponentsβandγarise from the asymptotic behavior of the dominant eigenvalue branch
λc(µ)∼(µ−µ c)ν,(797)
together with the associated eigenmode amplitude equation

In this representation, universality emerges from the fact thatβandγdepend only on the spectral degeneracy
class ofJ, and not on microscopic details of the climate subsystem.
Renormalization Group Interpretation.
Under a coarse-graining transformationR s, the system exhibits scale covariance:
Rs(P) =e κsP,(799)
implying that the dynamics evolve along renormalization group flow trajectories in operator space. Fixed points
of this flow satisfy
Rs(P∗) =P∗,(800)
and define universality classes of climate-like nonlinear systems.
Breakdown of Scale Invariance at Criticality.
At the bifurcation thresholdµ=µ c, the system exhibits emergent scale invariance:
X(t,ℓ)∼ℓ −∆X(t/ℓz),(801)
wherezis the dynamic critical exponent and ∆ is the scaling dimension of the climate state field.
This invariance implies that no intrinsic spatial or temporal scale dominates the dynamics, and all fluctuation
modes become self-similar across scales.
Universal Interpretation.
In the Universal Pellis framework, the scaling laws
δX∼|µ−µ c|β, τ∼|µ−µ c|−γ,(802)
are interpreted as manifestations of a deep spectral renormalization symmetry of the climate operator field. They
define universality classes of tipping behavior that are independent of microscopic parametrization and depend only
on the eigenstructure of the Pellis operator near marginal stability.
Thus, climate tipping points are not isolated dynamical anomalies but belong to a structured hierarchy of
universal critical phenomena governed by operator-level scaling laws in infinite-dimensional functional space.
6.8 Criticality as Spectral–Geometric Transition
Within the Universal Pellis framework, criticality emerges as a unified spectral–geometric phenomenon in which
operator instability, functional divergence, and phase-space reorganization become equivalent representations of the
same underlying transition in infinite-dimensional climate dynamics.
The onset of criticality is characterized by the simultaneous degeneration of the dominant spectral mode and the
blow-up of the linear response operator:
λmax(µ)→0,∥J −1(µ)∥→∞,(803)
whereλ max(µ) denotes the spectral abscissa of the linearized Pellis operatorJ(µ). This dual condition encodes
the collapse of exponential stability and the divergence of system susceptibility to infinitesimal perturbations.
Divergence of Linear Response and Susceptibility.
The response of the system to external forcingfis governed by
δX=J −1(µ)f.(804)
As the system approaches criticality, the norm of the response operator diverges:
∥δX∥∼∥J −1(µ)∥∥f∥→∞,(805)
which defines a singular amplification regime in which arbitrarily small perturbations can induce macroscopic
state transitions.
Resolvent Blow-Up and Spectral Singularity.
This instability can be equivalently formulated in terms of the resolvent operator
R(λ,J) = (J−λI) −1.(806)

∥R(λ,J(µ))∥→∞, λ→σ(J).(807)

of spectral collapse:
R(λ,J)∼ 1

Equivalence of Spectral, Geometric, and Dynamical Instability.
The critical transition admits three equivalent characterizations:
(Spectral)λ max(µ)→0,(809)
(Geometric) det(J(µ))→0,(810)
(Dynamical)∥J −1(µ)∥→∞.(811)
These conditions are mutually equivalent under the Fredholm framework of the Pellis operator and collectively
define the loss of structural stability in the climate state space.
Unified Interpretation.
Thus, climate tipping points correspond to a single unified phenomenon: a spectral–geometric transition in
which the linearized climate operator loses invertibility, the resolvent develops singular structure, and the system
becomes infinitely sensitive to perturbations.
Within this framework, spectral instability, geometric bifurcation, and observable climate tipping behavior
are not distinct mechanisms but different mathematical projections of the same underlying critical transition in the
Universal Pellis operator field.
7 Information-Theoretic and Entropic Structure
The information-theoretic structure of the climate system provides a unified framework connecting nonlinear
dynamical evolution, entropy production, and spectral instability. In this formulation, climate evolution is inter-
preted as a high-dimensional information flow governed by nonlinear operators and multiscale feedback mechanisms
consistent with dynamical systems theory [1–3], bifurcation theory [2, 3], and nonlinear stability analysis [3]. The
emergence of critical transitions is consistent with catastrophic shift theory [4,5,8], early warning signals [7,8,28,29],
and regime shift theory in Earth systems [6,9,27,33].
7.1 Information Measure of Climate States
Within the Universal Pellis framework, the climate system is naturally interpreted as a probability flow on an
infinite-dimensional functional manifoldH. Each realization of the Earth system stateX(t) is treated as a point in
a statistical ensemble governed by a time-dependent probability density functionalρ(X,t).

Physical Interpretation.

in unstable eigenmodes of the Pellis operator.

7.4 Fisher Information Geometry
Within the Universal Pellis framework, the space of climate probability statesρ(X,t) is endowed with a natural
differential-geometric structure induced by the Fisher information metric. This structure transforms the infinite-
dimensional state space into a Riemannian statistical manifold, where distances correspond to distinguishability
between infinitesimally perturbed climate configurations.
The Fisher metric tensor is defined as
gij =E[∂ i logρ(X)∂ j logρ(X)],(836)
where∂ i denotes functional differentiation with respect to thei-th coordinate direction in parameterized climate
state space.
Geometric Interpretation of Climate Sensitivity.
The metricg ij quantifies the sensitivity of the probability distribution to perturbations in control parameters.
In particular, small perturbationsδθ i induce a quadratic change in the statistical distance:
ds2 =g ijδθiδθj,(837)
which measures the local curvature of the climate information manifold.
Large values ofgij correspond to heightened susceptibility, where infinitesimal perturbations in forcing parameters
induce disproportionately large changes in the underlying probability structure.
Curvature and Critical Amplification.
The global geometric stability of the system is encoded in the scalar curvatureR g of the Fisher manifold. This
curvature is constructed from the Levi–Civita connection associated withg ij and captures second-order sensitivity
of statistical distinguishability.
At criticality, the curvature exhibits singular behavior:
Rg→∞,(838)
indicating a breakdown of smooth geometric structure in the space of climate states.
Breakdown of Linear Response Theory.
Divergence of Fisher curvature implies failure of linear response theory. The response function of an observable
Oto perturbations in a control parameterµis typically expressed as
χO(µ) = ∂⟨O⟩
∂µ .(839)

climate system.

linearized operatorJ(µ). In particular, the metric can be expressed in terms of eigenmodesψ λ as

Interpretation.
In physical terms, Fisher information geometry encodes the intrinsic ”shape” of climate sensitivity in parameter

up, and the manifold structure ceases to be locally Euclidean.
Within the Universal Pellis framework, this phenomenon represents a fundamental equivalence between spectral
instability, information-theoretic sensitivity, and geometric collapse of the climate state manifold, providing a unified
diagnostic signature of impending critical transitions.

7.7 Emergent Interpretation
Within the Universal Pellis framework, the climate system is interpreted as a high-dimensional information-
processing dynamical field in which thermodynamic, geometric, and spectral structures are not independent descrip-
tors but mutually coupled manifestations of a single underlying operator-driven evolution. In this view, entropy
production, Fisher information curvature, and spectral instability co-evolve under the action of the nonlinear Pellis
operatorP, forming an integrated hierarchy of stability constraints in functional state spaceH.

Unification of Theoretical Frameworks.
This emergent structure provides a unifying mathematical bridge between multiple theoretical domains:
•Dynamical systems theory, via spectral stability and operator evolution [1–3],
•Critical transition theory, via bifurcation manifolds and early-warning divergence [4–10],

Final Interpretation.
In summary, the Universal Pellis formulation identifies climate tipping points as universal information-theoretic
singularities in which geometry, entropy, and spectral stability coalesce into a single critical structure. These points
correspond to states of maximal information curvature and minimal predictability, where the climate system tran-
sitions from distributed multiscale variability to concentrated, low-dimensional instability dynamics governed by
dominant eigenmodes of the Pellis operator field.
8 Renormalization Structure of Climate Dynamics
The renormalization structure of climate dynamics formalizes the multiscale organization of the climate system as
a hierarchical flow of effective theories. Instead of treating climate variability as a single-scale nonlinear process, the
system is interpreted as an evolving field across resolution scales, where macroscopic regimes emerge from repeated
coarse-graining transformations. This perspective is grounded in renormalization group theory and the mathematical
structure of critical phenomena [18, 19, 30, 31], while its extension to complex geophysical and climate systems has
been widely developed in the context of tipping dynamics and cascading transitions [4–6,9,14,27].
8.1 Coarse-Graining and Scale-Dependent Climate States
The climate state is represented as a scale-dependent fieldX (s)∈M s, evolving under a nonlinear coarse-graining
operatorRacting on a hierarchy of effective state spaces. The renormalization transformation is defined by
X(s+1) =R(X (s)),(862)
wheres∈Ndenotes the resolution level andR:M s→M s+1 is generally non-invertible, inducing an information-
dissipative flow on the manifold of climate macrostates.
A standard realization ofRcan be written as a conditional expectation over unresolved microstatesx:
X(s) =E[x|Π s(x)],(863)

where Πs is a projection operator defining the retained degrees of freedom at scales. This induces a contraction of

8.3 Renormalization Covariance and Universality
The coupled climate–Pellis dynamics exhibit a fundamental covariance structure under coarse-graining, expressed
as a renormalization equivariance relation between the dynamical generator and the scale transformation operator.
At the level of state evolution, this reads
RP(X) =γP(RX),(884)
whereγ∈R + is a scaling exponent encoding the anomalous dimension of the dynamical flow under renormalization.
More generally, this relation defines a commutative diagram up to rescaling:
X P− →TM
↓R ↓γ
RX P− →TM
(885)
which encodes the covariance of the vector fieldPunder the renormalization semigroup action.
At the infinitesimal level, introducing the generatorLof the Pellis flow,
Lf(X) =∇f(X)·P(X),(886)
the covariance condition induces a conjugacy relation between operators:
R∗L=γLR ∗,(887)
whereR∗ denotes the induced action on observables. This implies thatLis an eigen-operator of the renormalization
transformation.
Consequently, the spectrum of linearized perturbations exhibits scaling self-similarity. LetδXsatisfy the
variational equation
d
dtδX=J P(X)δX.(888)
Under renormalization, the Jacobian transforms as
JP(RX) =γ−1 JP(X),(889)
implying that Lyapunov exponents scale according to
λ(s+1) =γ−1λ(s).(890)
This scaling structure defines a hierarchy of universality classesU γ, where systems sharing the same exponent
γexhibit identical macroscopic behavior under coarse-graining, despite differences in microscopic parameterization.
In particular, macroscopic observablesOobey asymptotic scaling laws of the form
O(ℓ)∼ℓ ∆O,(891)
whereℓdenotes the effective coarse-graining scale and ∆ O is the corresponding scaling dimension induced by the
renormalization flow.
At the level of probability measuresµ, universality emerges as invariance of the renormalized measure up to
normalization:
R#µ=Z −1µ,(892)
withZa normalization constant. Fixed points of this transformation satisfy
µ∗ =R #µ∗,(893)
and define critical climate regimes characterized by scale invariance and loss of characteristic length scales.
Near such fixed points, deviationsδµevolve according to
δµs+1 =LRδµs,(894)
whereLR is the linearized renormalization operator whose eigenvalues determine relevant, irrelevant, and marginal
directions in the space of climate dynamics.
The coexistence of dynamical evolution and renormalization covariance thus implies that climate trajectories
are organized into equivalence classes underR, with macroscopic observables governed solely by universal scaling
exponents rather than microscopic details. This establishes a rigorous correspondence between climate criticality
and classical renormalization group theory, consistent with scaling universality and critical phenomena in complex
systems [18,19,30,31].

8.6 Criticality and Structural Transitions in Renormalization Space
Critical transitions correspond to qualitative, topological reorganizations of the renormalization flow on the space
of effective climate states. In this framework, a tipping event is defined as a structural change in the fixed-point set
of the renormalization operator:
Fix(R) ={X∈M|R(X) =X},(912)
such that a transition satisfies
#Fix(R)−→#Fix(R ′)̸= #Fix(R),(913)
indicating a nontrivial bifurcation in the invariant set topology induced by coarse-graining.
More generally, criticality can be formulated as a loss of structural stability of the renormalization semigroup.
LetR ϵ denote a perturbed renormalization operator depending on a control parameterϵ. A critical transition occurs
when
lim
ϵ→ϵc
Rϵ̸∼R ϵc,(914)
in the sense of topological conjugacy breaking, implying that no homeomorphism preserves the dynamical structure
across the transition.
At the level of linearized dynamics, this corresponds to a qualitative change in the spectrum of the renormal-
ization Jacobian:
Spec(DRϵ)−→Spec(DR ϵ′),(915)
where eigenvalues cross the unit circle, inducing a reorganization of stable, unstable, and center manifolds.
At criticality, the system approaches a scale-invariant regime characterized by asymptotic invariance under
renormalization:
R(X)≃X,up to scaling transformations,(916)
which implies the emergence of universality and loss of characteristic scales.
This regime is accompanied by divergence of correlation length. LetC(r) denote a spatial correlation function
of a climate observable; near criticality it satisfies
C(r)∼r −ηe−r/ξ,(917)
where the correlation lengthξdiverges as
ξ∼|ϵ−ϵ c|−ν,(918)
withν >0 a critical exponent. The divergenceξ→∞signals the onset of system-wide coupling and breakdown of
local predictability.
Additionally, temporal fluctuations exhibit critical slowing down, expressed as a divergence of relaxation
timescales:
τ∼|ϵ−ϵ c|−κ,(919)
reflecting vanishing eigenvalues in the stability spectrum of the linearized renormalization flow.
From a geometric perspective, the critical point corresponds to a singularity in the renormalization flow
manifold where the effective metric becomes degenerate:
detg (R)
ij →0,(920)
indicating collapse of distinguishability between nearby macrostates.
These structural transitions unify bifurcation theory, renormalization group theory, and tipping-point phe-
nomena in complex Earth systems [4–8, 28, 29, 37, 38], establishing criticality as a geometric and spectral transition
in the space of renormalized climate dynamics.

9 Pellis Climate Index
The Pellis Climate Index (PCI) is introduced as a unified scalar functional designed to quantify proximity to
critical transitions in the climate system. It is constructed from the spectral properties of the Pellis operator,
the renormalization flow structure, and the information-theoretic entropy production of the underlying multiscale
dynamical field. The formulation extends classical early-warning indicators such as critical slowing down and variance
amplification [7, 8, 28, 29] into a fully operator-based instability metric grounded in spectral theory and nonlinear
dynamics [1–3,15,17].
98

===== PAGE 99 =====
9.1 Spectral Definition of the Pellis Climate Index
LetP:H→Hdenote the nonlinear Pellis operator acting on the infinite-dimensional climate state spaceH,

whereN λ[a] represents nonlinear mode coupling induced by triadic and higher-order interactions across the spectrum.

9.3 Renormalization-Enhanced Formulation
To incorporate multiscale effects, the Pellis Climate Index is promoted to a renormalization-indexed functional
hierarchy defined on the sequence of coarse-grained state spaces{X (s)}s∈N. At each resolution levels, the spectral
representation of the renormalized Pellis operatorP (s) yields

The renormalization structure induces a nontrivial flow on the space of stability functionals, which can be

At the level of spectral amplitudes, renormalization induces a transformation law of the form

where Λ(s)(λ) encodes linear amplification under coarse-graining andN (s) represents scale-dependent nonlinear spec-

whereC (s) aggregates higher-order mode coupling corrections induced by renormalization.
A scale-invariant regime is defined by the fixed-point condition in functional space:

implying that the stability functional becomes invariant under repeated coarse-graining transformations. Equiva-
lently, this requires

which identifies PCI∗ as a renormalization fixed functional.
At this fixed point, the spectral measure exhibits self-similarity of the form

Thus, scale invariance corresponds to a persistent critical regime in which the spectral instability measure
is invariant under hierarchical coarse-graining transformations, reflecting universality principles in renormalization
group theory and multiscale critical phenomena [18,19,30,31].

9.5 Threshold Structure and Tipping Condition
Within the spectral Pellis framework, a climate tipping point is formalized as a threshold crossing of the global
stability functional in which the balance between stable and unstable spectral contributions becomes degenerate.
The critical threshold is defined by the condition
PCI(tc) = 0,(969)
which identifies a neutral stability surface separating regimes of net decay and net amplification in the spectral energy
distribution.

threshold.
The transition across this manifold is determined by the directional derivative of the stability functional along

which encodes forward instability propagation and guarantees that trajectories crossing the threshold enter a regime

More generally, defining the gradient of the PCI in state space,

set of the functional:

This defines a codimension-one bifurcation manifold in the infinite-dimensional climate state space,
Mc ={X∈H|PCI(X) = 0},(974)
which acts as a separatrix between distinct dynamical regimes.
Crossing this manifold induces a qualitative change in the spectral structure of the Pellis operator, correspond-
ing to a bifurcation in the sign structure of its real spectrum and a reorganization of invariant subspaces.
This formulation is consistent with general bifurcation theory in nonlinear dynamical systems [2, 3, 14] and
with observed threshold behavior and abrupt transitions in coupled Earth system components [6, 9, 37, 38], where
tipping points manifest as sharp transitions between coexisting attractor basins.
9.6 Physical Interpretation and Planetary Instability
Within the spectral-operator formulation, the Pellis Climate Index provides a global diagnostic of the balance
between stabilizing and destabilizing dynamical modes distributed across the spectrum of the Pellis operator. In this
sense, the PCI acts as an integrated order parameter over multiscale climate interactions, aggregating local instability
mechanisms into a single global functional onH.
In particular, the PCI can be interpreted as a weighted spectral energy imbalance,
PCI(t) =
Z
σ(P)
Re(λ)|a(λ,t)| 2dµ(λ),(975)
which quantifies the net tendency of the system toward exponential growth or decay across all resolved and unresolved
dynamical modes.
From this perspective, high PCI regimes correspond to spectral configurations in which the unstable sector
dominates the global energy distribution:
Z
σ+
|a(λ,t)| 2dµ(λ)≫
Z
σ−
|a(λ,t)| 2dµ(λ),(976)
implying that positive-real-part eigenmodes control the macroscopic evolution of the climate state.
Conversely, low or negative PCI values correspond to regimes where dissipative spectral components suppress
growth, enforcing attraction toward stable invariant sets and maintaining robustness of the global climate attractor
structure.
The physical interpretation of dominant spectral modes can be further refined by decomposing the operator
spectrum into process-relevant subspaces:
σ(P) =σ AMOC∪σ cry∪σ bio∪σ atm,(977)
where each subset corresponds to dominant dynamical contributions from ocean circulation, cryospheric processes,
biospheric feedbacks, and atmospheric variability respectively.

===== PAGE 105 =====
In this decomposition, planetary instability arises when at least one subsystem spectral block undergoes a
transition in which its local contribution to the PCI becomes positive-definite in aggregate:
PCIα(t) =
Z

Planetary instability is therefore characterized not merely as a single-mode transition, but as a collective spec-
tral reorganization across interacting subsystems, in which feedback couplings amplify initially localized instabilities
into global-scale regime shifts.
This mechanism is consistent with cascading tipping dynamics observed in coupled Earth system components,
where instability propagates through interdependent feedback networks and leads to abrupt transitions between
climate attractors [27,32,33,37].
10 Early Warning Signals of Critical Transitions
The detection of early warning signals for critical transitions in the climate system is formulated as a spec-
tral–functional inference problem on the evolving stateX(t)∈Hgoverned by the Pellis operator dynamics intro-
duced in Sections 3 and 4. The central hypothesis is that proximity to a tipping point is not primarily encoded in
amplitude growth, but in structured changes in operator spectrum, autocorrelation geometry, and entropy production
rates [7,8,28,29,35,37].

of the spectral gap of the linearized stability operator. LetJdenote the Jacobian (Fr´ echet derivative) of the

This phenomenon is directly linked to critical slowing down in nonlinear dynamical systems, where recovery

10.8 Emergent Interpretation
Within the unified spectral–Pellis framework, early warning signals are interpreted as manifestations of a universal
spectral collapse mechanism governing the stability operatorP. In this regime, the approach to criticality is not
driven by a single observable, but by the coordinated degeneration of multiple statistical, dynamical, and information-
theoretic indicators induced by the collapse of the spectral gap.
Formally, the early-warning regime corresponds to a joint asymptotic structure in which the system exhibits
simultaneous scaling anomalies:
∆σ(t)→0 +, τ corr→∞,Var[X]→∞, dS
dt →max,(1024)
together with progressive eigenvalue migration toward the imaginary axis:
Re(λi(t))→0.(1025)
This co-evolution can be interpreted as a collapse of effective dimensionality in the spectral representation
of the climate state, where probability mass concentrates onto a shrinking set of near-marginal modes, producing
strong statistical dependence and long-range temporal memory.
::contentReference[oaicite:0]index=0
From an operator-theoretic perspective, this regime corresponds to a loss of spectral separation inσ(P), leading
to the breakdown of exponential mixing and the emergence of slow manifolds governing the macroscopic dynamics.
The system effectively transitions from a strongly contracting semigroup to a near-critical, weakly dissipative regime.
The simultaneous occurrence of slowing dynamics, variance amplification, entropy restructuring, and eigenvalue
migration defines a coherent pre-critical structure that is invariant under coarse-graining transformations, thereby
constituting a universality class of early-warning behavior shared across climate subsystems.
This universality interpretation is consistent with the notion that tipping behavior is not subsystem-specific but
instead emerges from a shared spectral mechanism embedded in the global Pellis operator. In this view, seemingly
distinct Earth system components exhibit qualitatively similar critical behavior due to the underlying coherence
of their spectral instability structure. This perspective is further supported by general results in the theory of
critical transitions and complex systems, which emphasize the emergence of universal behavior across heterogeneous
dynamical systems [6,7,9,33,37].
11 AMOC Stability and Collapse Mechanisms
The Atlantic Meridional Overturning Circulation (AMOC) is modeled as a coupled nonlinear transport–diffusion
subsystem embedded in the global climate fieldX(t)∈H. Within the Pellis Functional Operator framework, AMOC
dynamics are interpreted as a constrained projection of the full operator evolution onto a reduced but spectrally
dominant submanifold of ocean–thermohaline modes. Its stability is therefore governed by the restriction of the
global operatorPto an AMOC-invariant subspace [11–13,35–37].

gradient). The reduced dynamics are written as:

whereP A is the intrinsic AMOC operator andFA represents coupling with the full climate field, including cryospheric
and atmospheric feedback forcing.

11.2 Salt-Advection Feedback Nonlinearity
Within the thermohaline subsystem formulation, the dominant nonlinear mechanism governing AMOC stability
is the salt–advection feedback loop. This mechanism can be represented as a nonlinear operator acting on the state
vectorX A:
NS(XA) =α SS·v−β S∇S,(1036)
whereS(x,t) denotes salinity,v(x,t) is the meridional velocity field,α S quantifies advective reinforcement of salinity
anomalies, andβ S encodes diffusive smoothing of spatial gradients.
This nonlinear coupling induces a self-reinforcing transport loop in which salinity anomalies are both advected
by the circulation and simultaneously act to modify density gradients that drive the flow itself. In reduced form,

11.6 Coupled Cryosphere–AMOC Feedback Instability
Within the coupled climate–Pellis framework, cryospheric mass loss acts as an external slow-timescale forcing

11.8 Emergent Interpretation
Within the unified thermohaline–Pellis framework, AMOC collapse is interpreted as a coupled spectral catastrophe
occurring in the infinite-dimensional climate state spaceH. In this regime, nonlinear feedback amplification, spectral
migration of the stability operator, and cross-scale forcing interactions cease to be separable processes and instead
coalesce into a single operator-level bifurcation of the global climate field.
Formally, this coalescence can be expressed as a simultaneous degeneration of the spectral structure ofJ A and
the nonlinear feedback functionalG S:
α(JA(t))→0,Re(λ dom)→0, d
dtGS(t)>0,(1093)
indicating that both linear contraction and nonlinear damping mechanisms fail concurrently along the system tra-
jectory.
In this coupled regime, the effective evolution operator may be represented as a renormalized composite
generator:
Leff(t) =J A(t) +NS(t) +K cross(t),(1094)
whereK cross encodes multiscale interactions between thermohaline dynamics, cryospheric forcing, and large-scale
atmospheric variability. The collapse transition is then characterized by a loss of hyperbolicity ofL eff:
det
 
Leff−λI

→0 withλ≈0.(1095)
This mechanism implies that AMOC collapse is not reducible to a single dominant instability mode, but instead
arises from a coherent spectral reorganization in which multiple eigenmodes simultaneously approach marginality
while nonlinear feedback terms reinforce their growth.
119

===== PAGE 120 =====
From this perspective, the tipping event corresponds to a global bifurcation of the Pellis climate field:
P:X7→X ′,(1096)
in which the attractor structure of the coupled Earth system undergoes a discontinuous reconfiguration due to the
collapse of spectral separation across scales.
This provides a unified mathematical explanation of abrupt thermohaline shutdown events as emergent spec-
tral–nonlinear transitions in a coupled operator field, consistent with observed AMOC variability and broader indi-
cators of global climate instability [6,11,12,37].
12 Cryospheric Instability: Greenland Ice Sheet Dynamics
The Greenland Ice Sheet (GrIS) is modeled as a nonlinear thermo-mechanical subsystem embedded in the global
Pellis climate fieldX(t)∈H. Its dynamics are governed by coupled mass transport, basal sliding, meltwater perco-
lation, and radiative feedback mechanisms. Within the Universal Pellis Functional Operator framework, cryospheric
instability is interpreted as a spectral bifurcation of an ice-sheet evolution operator coupled to atmospheric and
oceanic forcing fields [4,6,7,9,33,37].
Let the cryospheric state be represented by:

whereP G is the intrinsic ice-sheet operator andF G encodes external coupling with oceanic and atmospheric modes.

12.5 Meltwater–Hydrological Feedback Network
Within the cryospheric–hydrological component of the coupled Pellis climate system, meltwater production and
transport are represented as a diffusion–source process driven by thermodynamic forcing from ice thickness and
temperature anomalies. The meltwater fieldM(x,t) satisfies
∂M
∂t =∇·(D∇M) +ηhT,(1133)
whereDdenotes the effective hydrological diffusivity capturing subglacial drainage and porous flow pathways, while
the source termηhTrepresents thermally activated melt production modulated by ice thickness and local tempera-
ture.
This formulation defines a nonlinear transport network in which meltwater is redistributed through spatial
gradients while simultaneously being generated by cryospheric thermal forcing. In particular, the coupling termηhT
induces a state-dependent amplification mechanism that links mechanical ice structure to hydrological production
rates.
The presence of basal meltwater modifies the effective frictional properties of the ice–bed interface, producing
a lubrication feedback that enhances sliding velocity. This is modeled as
vslide =v 0 +µM,(1134)
wherev 0 is the background sliding velocity in the absence of meltwater, andµ>0 quantifies the sensitivity of basal
slip to subglacial hydrological pressure.
Substituting this relation into the momentum balance of the ice-flow operator introduces a nonlinear coupling
between hydrology and mechanical deformation, effectively closing a positive feedback loop:
v∼ F(h,∇h) +µM.(1135)
The feedback strength of this coupling is characterized by the functional derivative
∂v
∂M =µ,(1136)
which is strictly positive under physically admissible parameter regimes. This implies that increases in meltwater
systematically enhance sliding velocity, thereby accelerating ice transport and further promoting melt production
through dynamic thinning.
In operator form, the coupled hydrological–mechanical system can be represented as a block-structured evo-
lution operator:
PMH =
PG CGM
CMG PM

whereC GM andC MG encode bidirectional coupling between ice dynamics and meltwater transport.
Instability arises when the coupling-induced amplification overcomes diffusive damping in the hydrological
subsystem, leading to a regime in which perturbations inMgrow and feed back into ice motion:
∂v
∂M >0⇒destabilizing coupling regime.(1138)
Spectrally, this corresponds to the emergence of additional positive real parts in the eigenvalue spectrum of
PMH due to off-diagonal coupling, effectively reducing the stability margin of the combined cryosphere–hydrology
operator:
Re(λeff)↑0 +.(1139)
Consequently, the meltwater–hydrological network acts as an internal amplification architecture that accel-
erates cryospheric thinning and enhances susceptibility to large-scale ice-sheet instability within the broader Pellis
climate criticality framework.

12.8 Emergent Interpretation
Within the unified cryospheric–Pellis framework, ice-sheet instability is interpreted as a genuinely multiscale spec-
tral collapse phenomenon in which thermo-mechanical deformation, hydrological lubrication, and radiative feedback
mechanisms cease to act as separable processes and instead merge into a single coupled operator dynamics. In this
regime, the effective evolution is governed by a composite nonlinear operator
Lcry(t) =P G(t) +PT (t) +PMH (t),(1173)
whose spectral structure encodes the full interaction between ice-flow rheology, thermal diffusion, and meltwater
transport.
The onset of instability corresponds to a global reorganization of the spectrum ofL cry, characterized by the
simultaneous migration of multiple eigenmodes toward marginal stability:
Re(λ(cry)
k )→0 +,∀k∈K dom,(1174)
whereK dom denotes the set of dynamically dominant cryospheric modes.
This spectral condensation implies a loss of scale separation, such that energy, mass, and momentum transfers
across scales become strongly coupled:
⟨ϕi,PMHϕj⟩̸= 0∀i,j∈K dom,(1175)
indicating the emergence of coherent cross-mode reinforcement rather than independent decay of perturbations.
In this regime, thermo-mechanical thinning, basal lubrication, and radiative amplification form a closed non-
linear feedback loop that can be represented schematically as
h−→T−→M−→v slide −→h,(1176)
constituting a self-reinforcing cascade embedded within the operator structure of the system.
The resulting bifurcation is therefore not local but global in nature, corresponding to a collapse of the invariant
manifold structure of the cryospheric state space:
Mstable −→ ∅,(1177)
as attractors associated with long-term ice-sheet persistence lose stability.
From the Pellis spectral viewpoint, this transition is equivalent to a catastrophic deformation of the eigenvalue
distribution of the coupled operator field, producing a universal shift toward marginal modes:
ρLcry(λ)→δ(λ−0),(1178)
where spectral weight accumulates near zero growth rate.
This mechanism provides a unified mathematical explanation for irreversible Greenland Ice Sheet retreat
as a multiscale operator bifurcation, in which interacting physical feedbacks collectively drive the system across
a critical threshold. In the broader planetary context, this cryospheric collapse acts as a major contributor to
coupled Earth system instability through its coupling to ocean circulation, sea-level rise, and radiative balance
feedbacks [4,6,9,33,37].
13 Arctic Sea Ice Regime Transitions
The Arctic sea ice system constitutes a canonical cryospheric subsystem characterized by strong nonlinear feed-
backs, threshold-driven instability, and abrupt regime transitions under external radiative forcing. Its dynamics
emerge from the interaction of thermodynamic phase change, ocean–atmosphere coupling, and multiscale energy
redistribution processes, forming a high-dimensional dissipative structure embedded within the global climate sys-
tem [6,7,9,33,37].
Within the Universal Pellis Function framework, Arctic sea ice dynamics are represented as a constrained
spectral subsystem of the global nonlinear operatorP, where stability is determined by spectral properties rather
than classical scalar energy balance arguments.
13.1 Thermodynamic–Radiative Feedback Structure
Within the coupled cryosphere–atmosphere component of the Pellis climate framework, sea ice extentA(t) and
surface temperature anomalyT s(t) evolve as a nonlinear feedback system driven by thermodynamic relaxation,
phase-change thresholds, and radiative forcing modulation. The sea ice balance equation is given by

The albedo feedback is modeled through a saturating exponential dependence on sea ice extent:

13.3 Energy Balance and Collapse Threshold
Within the Arctic–cryospheric–Pellis energetic formulation, the net energy balance is expressed as a nonlinear
competition between radiative absorption, outgoing longwave loss, and advective oceanic heat transport. The total
Arctic energy budget can be written as
EArctic = (1−α albedo(A))Qin−Qout + Φocean(A,Ts),(1198)
whereα albedo(A) encodes the state-dependent ice–albedo feedback,Q in is incoming shortwave radiation,Q out repre-
sents outgoing longwave radiative losses, and Φocean denotes nonlinear advective and turbulent ocean heat transport
into the Arctic domain.
To explicitly highlight feedback structure, the oceanic heat flux can be decomposed into a mean transport
component and a state-dependent amplification term:
Φocean(A,Ts) = Φ0 +χ 1Ts +χ 2(1−A)T s,(1199)
where reduced ice cover enhances ocean–atmosphere heat exchange through increased open-water exposure.

In particular, when the curvature term approaches zero, the system transitions from a locally stabilizing energy
