Thus, the Arctic energy balance acts as a macroscopic projection of the underlying spectral instability structure,
linking radiative forcing, ocean heat transport, and ice–albedo feedback into a unified collapse threshold within the
planetary Pellis criticality manifold [4,5,8,9].
13.4 Pellis Operator Projection
Within the full nonlinear climate–Pellis operator formalism, the cryospheric subsystem is obtained as a geometric
projection of the global state spaceHonto a reduced manifold of ice-relevant degrees of freedom. This is achieved
through the idempotent projection operator Π ice, yielding the restricted cryospheric evolution operator
Pice = ΠicePΠice,(1206)
wherePdenotes the full coupled climate evolution operator acting onH.
132

===== PAGE 133 =====
The projection Πice induces a decomposition of the global state vector into resolved and unresolved components:
X=X ice +X⊥,X ice = ΠiceX,X ⊥ = (I−Π ice)X,(1207)
which explicitly separates cryospheric dynamics from all complementary atmospheric, oceanic, and biospheric con-
tributions.
Under this decomposition, the reduced evolution equation takes the form
d
dtXice =P ice(Xice) +Ccoupling(Xglobal),(1208)
whereC coupling represents the non-closed forcing induced by unresolved and cross-scale interactions with the full
climate state.
In particular, the coupling term can be expressed as a back-projection of unresolved dynamics:
Ccoupling = ΠiceP(I−Π ice)X,(1209)
highlighting that cryospheric evolution is inherently driven by information flux from eliminated degrees of freedom.
This structure implies that the cryospheric subsystem is not dynamically closed, since
[P,Πice]̸= 0,(1210)
and therefore the projection operator does not commute with the full climate evolution operator. This non-
commutativity encodes irreversible information transfer across scales.
The effective reduced dynamics can thus be rewritten in memory-dependent form:

whereK(t) is a memory kernel induced by unresolved climate feedbacks.
From this perspective, the cryospheric operator is inherently non-Markovian, with effective dynamics shaped
by accumulated influence from eliminated atmospheric and oceanic modes.
Spectrally, projection induces a deformation of the original climate operator spectrum:
σ(Pice)⊂σ(P)⊕δσ coupling,(1212)
whereδσ coupling represents eigenvalue shifts generated by unresolved-scale feedback injection.
Consequently, stability properties of the cryospheric subsystem cannot be understood independently of the
global system, since cross-scale coupling continuously renormalizes the effective operator.
In the Pellis framework, this projection structure formalizes the emergence of multiscale instability: cryospheric
tipping behavior arises precisely because the reduced operatorP ice inherits destabilizing spectral contributions from
the full climate operatorPthrough non-closed coupling pathways, embedding ice dynamics within a globally inter-
connected instability field.

13.6 Coupling to Global Climate Instability Modes
Within the unified Pellis climate operator framework, the Arctic subsystem does not evolve in isolation but is

dynamics. The effective nonlinear coupling functional can be expressed as

13.7 Emergent Interpretation
Within the Universal Pellis Function framework, Arctic sea ice dynamics are interpreted as a canonical realization
of spectral instability in high-dimensional nonlinear dissipative systems. The evolution of the cryospheric state is
governed not by isolated thermodynamic relaxation alone, but by the global spectral structure of the coupled Earth
system operatorJ global, whose eigenmodes encode multiscale interactions across atmosphere, ocean, cryosphere, and
biosphere.
Regime transitions arise when the spectral distribution of this operator undergoes a controlled but irreversible
deformation, characterized by eigenvalue migration across the stability boundary:
Re(λk)<0−→Re(λ k)≥0,(1229)
for a nontrivial subset of modesk∈ K crit, indicating a loss of exponential decay and the onset of sustained or
amplifying perturbation growth.
Equivalently, this process can be expressed in terms of the spectral measureµ t associated withJ global, where
instability corresponds to a redistribution of spectral mass toward marginal modes:
µt(Re(λ)≈0)↑µ t(Re(λ)<0),(1230)
signaling a collapse of the damping-dominated regime and emergence of long-lived coherent structures.
In this setting, Arctic sea ice does not function as an isolated subsystem but rather as a low-dimensional
projection of a high-dimensional operator field:
A(t) =⟨X global(t),ϕice⟩,(1231)
whereϕ ice is an ice-sensitive eigenfunctional of the global climate state space. Instability inA(t) therefore reflects
instability in the projection of the full spectral hierarchy.
The resulting transition can be formalized as a global operator bifurcation, in which the climate system
undergoes a qualitative change in its invariant manifold structure:
Mstable
climate −→ Mreorganized
climate ,(1232)
accompanied by a reconfiguration of attractor basins and loss of long-term structural persistence.
From the Pellis perspective, this mechanism elevates cryospheric tipping from a localized thermodynamic
instability to a global spectral phase transition in an infinite-dimensional dynamical system. The essential feature is
not merely the amplitude of ice loss, but the migration of eigenstructure across stability boundaries, which reorganizes
the effective dimensionality and coherence of the planetary state space.
In this sense, Arctic sea ice acts as a sensitive indicator and active amplifier of planetary-scale spectral
reorganization, providing a mathematically coherent pathway linking local nonlinear feedbacks to global Earth system
instability.

14 Amazon Rainforest Dieback
The Amazon rainforest constitutes a nonlinear biospheric subsystem operating near critical hydrological and
carbon-cycle thresholds. Its stability emerges from coupled interactions between evapotranspiration, atmospheric
moisture recycling, soil respiration, and radiative forcing feedbacks. In the context of Earth system dynamics, it be-
haves as a metastable attractor embedded within a higher-dimensional climate operator field, exhibiting susceptibility
to abrupt dieback transitions under sustained perturbation [6,9,33,37,38].
Within the Universal Pellis Function framework, the Amazon system is represented as a multiscale coupled
field interacting with atmospheric and oceanic components through nonlinear feedback operators. Regime collapse
is interpreted as a spectral instability of the coupled biosphere–climate operator rather than a purely ecological
degradation process.
14.1 Hydrological–Carbon Feedback Structure
Within the coupled biosphere–hydrosphere component of the Pellis climate system, forest biomass densityF(t)
and atmospheric humidity recycling efficiencyH(t) constitute a strongly nonlinear feedback pair embedded in a
higher-dimensional climate operator field. Their joint evolution is governed by a modified reaction–diffusion–feedback
structure that integrates ecological growth, hydrological recycling, and disturbance-driven mortality.

14.5 Pellis Operator Projection onto Biospheric Manifold
Within the global Pellis climate operator framework, the Amazon subsystem is obtained through a geometric

As a consequence, stability properties of the Amazon subsystem cannot be decoupled from the global cli-

This establishes the Amazon biosphere as a dynamically open subsystem embedded in a globally coupled
operator field, where nonlinear feedbacks and cross-scale interactions continuously renormalize its effective evolution
and determine its proximity to tipping behavior.

14.7 Emergent Interpretation
Within the Universal Pellis Function framework, Amazon rainforest dieback is interpreted as a manifestation
of biosphere-level spectral instability embedded in the coupled biosphere–atmosphere–climate operator field. The
system does not undergo a purely local ecological degradation process; instead, it experiences a global redistribution
of spectral weight across interacting operators governing vegetation, hydrology, and radiative forcing.
At the operator level, the full Earth system generatorPadmits a coupled decomposition
P=P A +Pclimate +Pcoupling,(1280)

As a consequence, rainforest stability is reinterpreted not as an isolated ecological property but as a projection

Thus, Amazon dieback emerges as a functional phase collapse of the biosphere–atmosphere operator field,

15 Monsoon System Transitions
The monsoon system constitutes a nonlinear, seasonally forced atmospheric–oceanic dynamical subsystem char-
acterized by abrupt transitions between active and suppressed precipitation regimes. Its dynamics emerge from the
interaction of land–sea thermal contrast, moisture transport, convective instability, and large-scale circulation modes,
forming a forced dissipative system with intrinsic threshold behavior and hysteresis effects [11,14,27–29,33,37].
Within the Universal Pellis Function framework, the monsoon system is interpreted as a periodically driven
spectral subsystem of the global climate operatorP, where regime transitions correspond to instability-induced
reorganization of dominant spectral modes under seasonal forcing.
15.1 Thermodynamic Forcing and Moisture Transport Dynamics
Within the coupled atmosphere–land–ocean subsystem of the Pellis climate framework, monsoon precipitation
intensityM(t) and land–sea thermal contrast ∆T(t) constitute a dynamically coupled nonlinear pair that governs
large-scale moisture transport, convective organization, and hydrological amplification feedbacks.
The evolution of monsoon strength is governed by a nonlinear growth–saturation equation with convective

Within the Universal Pellis Function framework, this transition corresponds to a spectral reconfiguration of

A transition into an active monsoon or deep convection regime occurs when CAPE crosses a critical threshold:
CAPE(M,q)>CAPE c,(1297)
which defines a nonlinear activation manifold in the (M,q) state space separating suppressed convection from self-
sustaining convective organization.
Near the threshold, the system exhibits a sharp bifurcation in convective response intensity, which can be
expressed as an effective activation function:
Mconv =M 0·Θ(CAPE−CAPE c),(1298)
where Θ denotes a smooth limiting sigmoid approximation of the Heaviside function, capturing the rapid onset of
deep convection once buoyancy forcing exceeds stability constraints.
The coupled moisture–convection system thus admits a bistable structure characterized by competing attrac-
tors: a dry, suppressed-convection regime and a wet, self-amplifying convective regime. These states are separated
by a saddle-node bifurcation manifold defined implicitly by

which marks the collapse of intermediate stability states.
In dynamical systems terms, this bistability arises from nonlinear feedback between moisture accumulation,
latent heat release, and vertical buoyancy generation, producing hysteresis in the monsoon transition. Consequently,
once the system crosses the convective threshold, return to the dry regime requires substantially stronger negative
forcing, reflecting irreversible memory effects in the coupled atmospheric operator.
Within the Pellis operator framework, this behavior corresponds to a local spectral reorganization in which
convective eigenmodes transition from damped to growing states as the leading eigenvalue satisfies
Re(λmax)→0 +,(1300)
signaling proximity to a convective tipping manifold embedded within the global climate operator spectrum.
15.3 Spectral Stability of Seasonal Forcing Dynamics
Within the periodically forced monsoon subsystem, the full state vectorX M = (M,∆T,q) evolves under a time-
dependent nonlinear operator whose linearization about a periodic orbit yields
∂tδXM =J M(t)δXM,(1301)
whereJ M(t+T) =J M(t) is a time-periodic Jacobian operator induced by seasonal modulation of radiative forcing,
land–sea thermal contrast, and moisture recycling intensity.
The stability of such periodically driven dynamical systems is governed not by instantaneous eigenvalues, but
by Floquet theory. The monsoon perturbation evolution over one full seasonal cycle defines the monodromy operator
U(T) such that
δXM(t+T) =U(T)δX M(t),(1302)
whereU(T) is the time-ordered exponential

Near this boundary, the dominant Floquet exponentρ i = log|µi|approaches zero from below,

Within the Universal Pellis Function framework, this corresponds to a spectral transition in which the time-
periodic Pellis operator develops marginal Floquet modes, signaling proximity to a seasonal tipping manifold where
periodic forcing ceases to stabilize the coupled moisture–thermal dynamics [28,29].
15.4 Energy Balance and Latent Heat Feedback
Within the monsoon–thermodynamic subsystem, the total energy budget is governed by the interplay between
latent heat release, sensible heat storage, radiative losses, and ocean–atmosphere exchange fluxes. The effective
monsoon energy functional is therefore written as
EM =L vM+C p∆T−R out + Φocean−atm + Φlatent−coupling,(1309)
whereL vMrepresents latent heat release proportional to precipitation intensity,C p∆Tis the sensible heat contri-
bution from land–sea thermal contrast, and Φ latent−coupling encodes higher-order nonlinear energy transfer between
phase-change processes and atmospheric circulation.
To explicitly resolve feedback amplification pathways, the latent coupling term can be expanded as
Φlatent−coupling =η 1M∆T+η 2Mq−η 3M 3,(1310)
where the cubic term represents nonlinear saturation due to convective self-limitation and atmospheric moisture
depletion at extreme precipitation intensities.
The temporal evolution of monsoon energy is governed by a nonlinear balance law:
dEM
dt =λ 1M−λ 2M 2 +λ 3∆TM+λ 4Mq−λ 5∆T 2,(1311)
which captures the competition between latent heat amplification, nonlinear damping, and thermally driven desta-
bilization.
The termλ 1Mrepresents primary latent heat injection through condensation, whileλ 3∆TMencodes thermo-
dynamic reinforcement of convection via land–sea contrast. The quadratic term−λ 2M 2 reflects nonlinear saturation
due to moisture limitation and convective breakup, and higher-order corrections introduce additional stabilizing and
destabilizing nonlinearities.
The system exhibits a characteristic saturation-driven instability mechanism when the effective growth rate
becomes state-dependent:
λeff (M,∆T,q) =λ 1 +λ 3∆T+λ 4q−λ 2M,(1312)
leading to a transition from linear amplification to nonlinear suppression as precipitation intensity increases.
The tipping condition is obtained from the loss of monotonic energy growth:
dEM
dt = 0, d2EM
dt2 <0,(1313)
defining a saturation-induced bifurcation manifold separating stable monsoon operation from collapse regimes.
In the strongly forced regime, latent heat feedback dominates radiative damping, producing runaway convective
organization followed by abrupt structural breakdown once moisture reservoirs are exhausted.
Within the Pellis operator framework, this corresponds to a nonlinear renormalization of the effective energy
operator, where latent heat coupling shifts dominant spectral modes toward marginal stability, signaling proximity
to a thermodynamic tipping manifold in the coupled Earth system [27,32,33,37].

15.5 Pellis Operator Projection onto Monsoon Manifold
Within the Universal Pellis Function framework, the monsoon subsystem is rigorously defined as a geometric
reduction of the full Earth system operatorPonto a lower-dimensional invariant submanifoldMassociated with
atmospheric convection, thermodynamic gradients, and moisture transport processes. This projection is implemented
via the operator ΠM, yielding the reduced monsoon generator
PM = ΠMPΠM,(1314)
where ΠM acts as a nonlinear filtering operator that retains only convective–thermodynamic degrees of freedom while
eliminating fast radiative and oceanic modes.
The full climate state vector admits the decomposition
X=X M +X⊥,X M = ΠMX,X ⊥ = (I−Π M)X,(1315)
explicitly separating monsoon-relevant dynamics from unresolved global climate components.
Under this decomposition, the reduced evolution equation is given by
d
dtXM =P M(XM) +Ctele(Xglobal),(1316)
whereC tele represents nonlocal teleconnection forcing terms arising from coupled ocean–atmosphere modes, including
ENSO variability, Walker circulation shifts, and extratropical wave propagation.
To explicitly expose the non-closure of the projected system, the teleconnection operator can be written as a
back-projection of unresolved global dynamics:
Ctele = ΠMP(I−Π M)X,(1317)
demonstrating that monsoon evolution is continuously driven by feedback from external climate reservoirs.
The resulting system is therefore intrinsically non-autonomous, with effective forcing structure
d

whereK tele(t) is a memory kernel encoding delayed teleconnection responses across oceanic and atmospheric waveg-
uides.
At the spectral level, projection induces a deformation of the monsoon operator spectrum:
σ(PM) = ΠMσ(P)ΠM +δσ tele,(1319)
whereδσ tele represents eigenvalue shifts induced by ENSO-driven and global circulation-mediated coupling.
Instability emerges when teleconnection-induced spectral drift drives dominant modes toward marginal sta-
bility:
Re(λ(M)

signaling that the monsoon subsystem is no longer dynamically autonomous but instead locked into a globally coupled
instability regime. This establishes the monsoon manifold as a strongly non-closed subsystem whose stability is
fundamentally governed by global-scale spectral coupling within the Pellis climate operator hierarchy [28,29,37].
15.6 Regime Transition as Functional Phase Change
Within the monsoon manifoldM, regime transitions are interpreted as singular perturbations of the invariant
measure associated with the reduced Pellis operatorP M. In this formulation, the system does not undergo a smooth
deformation of trajectories, but rather a discontinuous reorganization of the underlying ergodic structure in phase
space, encoded as
∆µ(σ(PM))̸= 0,(1321)
whereµdenotes the invariant spectral measure induced by the long-time dynamics of the operator semigroup gen-

To connect microscopic variability with macroscopic switching, the regime indicator function may be written

where Θ is the Heaviside activation function emerging as the sharp-limit representation of smooth probabilistic

Within the Universal Pellis Function framework, this regime transition is therefore interpreted as a functional

migration across stability boundaries and irreversible loss of intermediate metastable states [28,29].
15.7 Emergent Interpretation
Within the Universal Pellis Function framework, monsoon regime transitions are interpreted as forced nonlinear
spectral bifurcations in a periodically driven dissipative operator system. The governing dynamics are not reducible
to smooth parameter drift, but instead emerge from structural instabilities in the spectrum of the global climate
generatorPwhen restricted to the monsoon manifoldM.
In this setting, seasonal forcing acts as a periodic modulation of the effective operator:
PM(t+T) =P M(t),(1330)
inducing a Floquet structure in the associated evolution semigroup. The long-term dynamics are therefore governed
by the monodromy operatorU(T), whose spectral properties determine regime stability:
U(T)ϕi =µ iϕi, µ i∈σ F (PM).(1331)
A monsoon transition is then identified with a Floquet-induced eigenvalue crossing event, in which at least
one multiplier traverses the unit stability boundary:
|µi(t)|↑1→ |µ i(t)|>1.(1332)
147

===== PAGE 148 =====
This crossing marks the breakdown of bounded periodic response and the onset of exponentially amplified inter-annual
variability.
At the infinitesimal level, this corresponds to a collision of Floquet exponents in the complex plane:
ρi = 1
T log(µi),Re(ρ i)→0 +,(1333)
signaling marginal destabilization of the seasonal attractor embedded in the climate operator spectrum.
In spectral terms, the phenomenon can be interpreted as a redistribution of eigenvalue density under periodic
forcing:
σ(PM(t))−→σ(P M(t)) +δσ Floquet,(1334)
whereδσ Floquet encodes forcing-induced deformation of the global climate spectrum through resonance with seasonal
harmonics.
Consequently, monsoon variability is not merely a regional hydrological fluctuation but a dynamically embed-
ded component of the global instability cascade. Seasonal forcing injects energy into specific spectral bands of the
Pellis operator, which then propagate through nonlinear coupling channels into cross-scale climate modes, amplifying
sensitivity to perturbations.
This leads to a unified interpretation in which monsoon tipping behavior is a manifestation of planetary-scale
spectral coherence breakdown: a regime where Floquet structure, nonlinear feedback, and global operator geometry
coalesce to produce abrupt hydrological phase reorganization [28,29,37].
16 Planetary Instability Field Theory
The Planetary Instability Field Theory formalizes the Earth system as a coupled, nonlinear, multiscale instability
field evolving on a high-dimensional functional manifold. In contrast to classical equilibrium-based formulations,
planetary behavior is represented as an emergent consequence of spectral deformation, renormalization flow collapse,
and global bifurcation structure in operator space. The resulting framework extends the Pellis Functional Operator
Formalism into a fully planetary-scale instability theory, where climate subsystems interact as nonlinearly coupled
critical fields [1–139].

a local instability phenomenon.

17 Functional Phase Transition Theory
This section develops a rigorous mathematical framework for functional phase transitions in infinite-dimensional
climate dynamics. The theory generalizes classical thermodynamic phase transitions to nonlinear operator-valued
evolution systems, where criticality emerges from spectral instability, renormalization flow singularities, and entropy-
driven bifurcation mechanisms. The formulation connects nonlinear dynamics, statistical physics, and climate tipping
theory into a unified operator-theoretic structure [4–6,18,19,30,31].

17.2 Spectral Mechanism of Phase Transitions
Functional phase transitions arise from a restructuring of the spectral measure of the nonlinear evolution operator

17.4 Renormalization-Induced Criticality
Under a renormalization-group flowR:H→H, the state of the system evolves on a hierarchy of coarse-grained
representations:
X(n+1) =R(X (n)), n∈N,(1439)
where each iteration corresponds to a contraction of microscopic degrees of freedom into an effective macroscopic
state.
Fixed points of the flow define scale-invariant configurations:
R(X∗) =X∗,(1440)
which correspond to self-similar invariant measures under successive coarse-graining transformations.
Linearization around a fixed point yields the stability operator:
DR(X∗) :T X∗H→T X∗H,(1441)
governing the evolution of infinitesimal perturbations:
δX(n+1) =DR(X∗)δX (n).(1442)
Criticality emerges when the spectral radius of the linearized renormalization operator reaches unity:
ρ(DR(X∗)) = 1,(1443)
indicating marginal stability and the onset of scale invariance across all resolved levels of description.

This establishes renormalization-induced criticality as a universal mechanism of scale-transcending instability,
in which the fixed-point structure ofRdetermines the macroscopic universality class of the underlying dynamical
system [18,19,31].

17.6 Emergent Climate Interpretation of Phase Transitions
Functional phase transitions correspond to macroscopic reorganizations of the invariant measure structure and

In dynamical systems terms, they correspond to attractor bifurcations, basin boundary crises, or interior crises de-

At the operator level, such events are driven by loss of structural stability of the flow generatorP, typically
associated with spectral condensation and collapse of the spectral gap:
gap(σ(P))→0,(1459)
which induces long-range correlation growth and breakdown of effective scale separation.
Thus, functional phase transitions are universal features of nonlinear dynamical systems with coupled multi-
scale feedbacks, where cascading interactions propagate instability across spectral, geometric, and measure-theoretic
layers of the phase space structure [27,32,33].
18 Symmetry Breaking in Climate Dynamics
Symmetry breaking in climate dynamics provides the fundamental mechanism through which an initially homo-
geneous or statistically symmetric planetary state evolves toward heterogeneous, structured, and regime-separated
configurations. Within the Universal Pellis Function framework, symmetry is not merely geometric or physical but
functional and spectral: it is defined on the operator algebra governing multiscale climate evolution [1,2,6,18,19].
We consider the climate stateX(t)∈Hgoverned by the Pellis operator:
d
dtX(t) =P(X(t)),(1460)
wherePencodes nonlinear feedbacks, spectral interactions, and renormalization effects. Symmetry breaking emerges
whenPceases to commute with a symmetry groupGacting onH:
[P,G]̸= 0.(1461)
18.1 Initial Symmetry Manifold
LetM 0⊂Hdenote the symmetric climate manifold characterized as the fixed-point set of a Lie group (or more
generally, a symmetry semigroup)Gacting smoothly on the state space:
M0 = Fix(G) ={X∈H:GX=X,∀G∈G}.(1462)
Infinitesimally, symmetry invariance is generated by a Lie algebragwith generators{T a}such that:
TaX= 0,∀X∈M 0,(1463)
implying thatM 0 is a constraint submanifold defined by vanishing Noether charges associated with the symmetry
action.
In this regime, the energy functionalE:H→Ris invariant under the group action:
E[GX] =E[X],(1464)
160

===== PAGE 161 =====
which induces conserved quantities via the functional Noether map:
d
dtQa(X) = 0, Q a(X) =
δE
δX,TaX

.(1465)
Consequently, dynamics restricted toM 0 evolve on an invariant foliation ofH:
X(t)∈M 0 ⇒ P(X(t))∈T X(t)M0,(1466)
ensuring closure of the reduced dynamical system under symmetry-preserving evolution.
Statistically, this symmetry implies invariance of the induced invariant measureµ 0 under pushforward:
G#µ0 =µ 0,(1467)
yielding homogeneous macroscopic observables and suppression of large-scale pattern selection.
Linearization aroundM 0 exhibits a block-diagonal decomposition of the tangent space:
TXH=T XM0⊕N XM0,(1468)
whereN XM0 corresponds to symmetry-breaking directions.
The onset of instability can then be interpreted as spontaneous symmetry breaking:
G-invariance−→ G-broken phase,(1469)
triggered when eigenmodes associated withN XM0 acquire positive growth rates in the spectrum of the linearized
operatorL.
Thus, the initial climate regimeM 0 serves as a symmetry-protected phase in which macroscopic order param-
eters vanish, and all observed structure emerges only via subsequent bifurcation-driven symmetry breaking [4,5,22].

Consequently, symmetry stability is equivalent to spectral stability within symmetry-adapted subspaces, while

linking group-theoretic structure directly to the spectral geometry of the linearized climate operator [7,28,29].
18.3 Nonlinear Amplification of Symmetry Modes
In the fully nonlinear regime, symmetry breaking is no longer governed solely by the linear stability operator but
is instead driven by mode–mode interactions that redistribute energy across symmetry sectors. LetX(t) admit a
decomposition into symmetry-adapted components{X k}in an appropriate basis of irreducible representations. The
nonlinear evolution can then be expressed as:

whereDdenotes the dissipative component of the linearized operator (typically the stable part ofJrestricted to

multiple distinct perturbation directions become dynamically indistinguishable. This leads to a loss of structural
stability and the emergence of coherent macroscopic symmetry-breaking modes.
Thus, nonlinear amplification acts as the mechanism by which infinitesimal symmetry-breaking perturbations
are transferred across scales and sectors, converting local instabilities into global reorganizations of the climate state
manifold through triadic coupling cascades and operator degeneracy.

18.4 Bifurcation Structure of Symmetry Breaking
Letµ∈Rdenote a slow-varying control parameter representing effective external forcing (e.g., radiative imbalance,
boundary flux drift, or parameterized coupling intensity). In a symmetry-reduced normal form, the dynamics near
the bifurcation point can be expressed as a canonical amplitude equation on the critical eigenspace:
dX
dt =µX−B(X,X,X) +O(∥X∥ 5),(1483)
whereBis a symmetric trilinear form encoding the leading-order nonlinear self-interaction responsible for saturation
of linear growth.
In an isotropic symmetry class,Breduces to a scalar cubic nonlinearity, yielding the prototypical pitchfork
normal form: dX
dt =µX−∥X∥ 2X+O(∥X∥ 5),(1484)
which enforces rotational invariance in the reduced order-parameter space and guarantees symmetry-consistent sat-

Equilibrium solutions are obtained by solving:
µX∗−∥X∗∥2X∗ = 0,(1485)
leading to the bifurcation branches:
X∗(µ) = 0,or∥X ∗(µ)∥= √µ.(1486)
The trivial branchX ∗ = 0 corresponds to the symmetric phase, which is linearly stable forµ <0 and loses
stability at the critical thresholdµ c = 0. The nontrivial branches emerge forµ >0, forming a symmetry-broken
manifold of equilibria:
MSB(µ) ={X∗∈H:∥X ∗∥= √µ}, µ>0.(1487)
Linear stability of the equilibria is determined by the Jacobian of the flow:
J(X∗) =µI−3X ∗⊗X∗,(1488)
whose eigenstructure exhibits a single neutral direction at the bifurcation point and anisotropic contraction along
symmetry-selected modes forµ>0.
Thus, symmetry is preserved in the subcritical regimeµ <0, while spontaneous symmetry breaking occurs
forµ >0, where the system transitions from a unique invariant state to a continuous family of symmetry-related
equilibria parameterized by the emergent order parameter magnitude.
This pitchfork structure serves as the local normal form of climate symmetry breaking, embedding the onset
of macroscopic regime differentiation into a universal bifurcation geometry governed by the control parameterµand
its coupling to the nonlinear saturation operator [3,14,34].
18.5 Spectral Interpretation of Broken Symmetry
Within the Pellis spectral framework, the nonlinear evolution operatorPis characterized (in an appropriate rigged
Hilbert space extension) by the generalized eigenvalue problem:
Pϕλ =λϕ λ, λ∈σ(P),(1489)
whereσ(P)⊂Cdenotes the full spectrum, including point, continuous, and residual components.
A symmetry groupGacting onHinduces an equivariant action on spectral modes. In the symmetric phase,
the spectrum is invariant under this action in the sense of spectral conjugacy:
λ∈σ(P)⇒ Gλ∈σ(P),(1490)
ensuring degeneracy and isotropy of spectral contributions.
Symmetry breaking corresponds to a lifting of this degeneracy, producing a spectral splitting of the form:
σ(P)→σ 1∪σ 2,(1491)
whereσ 1 denotes the symmetry-invariant sector (closed underG), whileσ 2 corresponds to symmetry-irreducible or
symmetry-broken modes exhibiting anisotropic growth and nontrivial representation content.

===== PAGE 164 =====
In operator-theoretic terms, this splitting can be interpreted as a decomposition of the spectral measure:

withdµ 2 supported on modes that fail to remain invariant under the induced group action.
The spectral amplitudes decompose accordingly:
X(t) =

Within the Universal Pellis framework, symmetry breaking is not a secondary emergent effect but the primary
geometric and spectral mechanism underlying climate tipping phenomena. In operator-theoretic terms, the onset
of instability is initiated by the loss of commutativity between the evolution operator and the governing symmetry
group:

Therefore, within the Pellis operator framework, climate tipping points are rigorously interpreted as the non-

19 Network Theory of Planetary Coupling
The planetary climate system can be rigorously reformulated as a high-dimensional interacting network of cou-
pled dynamical subsystems, where each node represents a physically distinct component (atmosphere, ocean basin,
cryosphere, biosphere, and anthropogenic forcing modules), and edges encode nonlinear, scale-dependent energy,
mass, and information transfer pathways. Within the Universal Pellis Function framework, this network is not static
but evolves as a spectral–functional object governed by operator-valued adjacency dynamics [1,6,18,19,26,27,33].
We define the planetary climate network as a weighted graph:
GP = (V,E,W(t)),(1514)
whereVis the set of climatic subsystems,Eis the interaction structure, andW(t) is a time-dependent weight tensor
encoding coupling intensities.

19.4 Spectral Stability of Climate Networks
Within the operator-theoretic formulation of multilayer climate dynamics, global stability is governed by the
spectral properties of the generalized network LaplacianL(W) acting on the extended state spaceH. A natural
stability functional is therefore defined as the spectral abscissa:
Snet(X,t) = sup

Snet<0,(1536)
which ensures that all eigenmodes ofL(W) decay exponentially and that perturbations remain asymptotically
bounded inH.
Conversely, the onset of global instability is characterized by spectral neutralization, where the leading eigen-
value reaches the imaginary axis:
Snet = 0,(1537)
marking a loss of spectral gap and the emergence of marginally stable coherent modes spanning multiple network
layers.
More generally, near-critical regimes exhibit spectral condensation:
σ(L)→ {λ: Re(λ)≈0},(1538)
which induces critical slowing down, increased correlation length scales across the network, and enhanced sensitivity
to perturbations.
This defines a network-level tipping threshold manifold in state space:
Tnet ={X∈H: det(L(X)) = 0},(1539)
interpreted as the singular set of the generalized Laplacian operator, where rank deficiency corresponds to the collapse
of independent dynamical directions in the network flow.
Equivalently, the tipping condition may be expressed in spectral form as:
∃λ∈σ(L) such thatλ= 0,(1540)
indicating the emergence of a neutral mode that mediates long-range correlation and initiates global restructuring
of the climate network.
Thus, spectral stability in climate networks is fundamentally governed by the interplay between eigenvalue
migration, operator degeneracy, and topology-dependent coupling, with tipping behavior emerging precisely at the
boundary between strictly contractive and marginally stable spectral regimes.
19.5 Cascade Propagation and Systemic Risk
In the operator-theoretic formulation of climate network dynamics, perturbation evolution is governed by the
nonlinear variation-of-constants representation associated with the semigroup generated by the generalized Laplacian

The nonlinear term further modifies cascade propagation through state-dependent renormalization of effective

feedback loops, resulting in global-scale regime reconfiguration [27,32,33,37].
19.6 Network-Induced Critical Transitions
Within the multilayer Pellis network formalism, critical transitions arise not merely from parameter variation
in fixed dynamics, but from structural reconfiguration of the underlying interaction graph. LetG P (t) denote the
time-dependent climate network; a structural transition is characterized by:
∆GP =G P (t+)−G P (t−)̸= 0,(1544)
where the difference is understood in the sense of graph topology, adjacency measure, or equivalently in the operator-
theoretic sense of perturbations in the associated LaplacianL(t).

alized network operatorL(t), where previously dispersed spectral components aggregate near critical thresholds:

This condensation reflects a collapse of spectral gap structure, leading to enhanced mode degeneracy and the
emergence of coherent collective eigenmodes spanning multiple network layers. In this regime, the resolvent norm
diverges:

Thus, network-induced critical transitions represent structural phase changes in the climate interaction graph,
where topology, spectrum, and attractor geometry co-evolve, producing abrupt regime shifts that are mathemat-
ically analogous to phase transitions in statistical physics systems with long-range coupling and collective mode
synchronization [18,30,31].
19.7 Emergent Interpretation
The Network Theory of Planetary Coupling establishes that climate tipping points are not reducible to isolated
subsystem instabilities, but instead arise as global spectral–topological reorganizations of an evolving interaction
graphG P (t). In this framework, the climate system is interpreted as a co-evolving pair (X(t),G P (t)), where both
state and connectivity participate in a unified dynamical feedback loop governed by the Pellis operator formalism.
The embedded network structure is realized through the state-dependent operator familyL(W(t)), inducing

where critical behavior emerges when eigenvalues cross the stability boundary and accumulate near the imaginary
axis, inducing loss of hyperbolicity and amplification of coherent network modes.
In parallel, adjacency deformation introduces a structural contribution to instability through topology-driven
spectral feedback:

Collectively, these mechanisms imply that planetary instability should be interpreted as a coherent network-
level phase transition in which spectral geometry, adjacency structure, and nonlinear dynamics co-evolve toward
criticality. In this sense, tipping points correspond to emergent global reorganizations of the interaction graph,
driven by nonlinear coupling amplification across scales and mediated by the spectral dynamics of the Pellis operator
[6,18,27,33,139].
20 Variational Formulation of Climate Evolution

tionary condition of an action functional defined over an infinite-dimensional state manifold. Within the Universal

dynamics into a single extremal principle [1,2,5,18,19,22].

nonlinear feedback-induced deviations from pure gradient relaxation.
In this representation, the climate system evolves as a non-Euclidean gradient flow onH, where the geometry
of the state space is dynamically shaped byM(X), and instability arises when nonlinear forcing distorts the descent
direction away from the steepest-energy trajectory.
Thus, the Euler–Lagrange formulation provides a rigorous variational foundation for climate dynamics, con-
necting inertial structure, energy landscapes, and nonlinear forcing into a unified functional framework that reduces
in the overdamped limit to a geometry-dependent gradient flow governing large-scale Earth system evolution.
20.3 Connection to the Pellis Operator
The variational formulation derived from the climate action functional can be recast in a unified operator-theoretic
form. In particular, the overdamped Euler–Lagrange dynamics correspond to a generalized gradient flow perturbed
by nonlinear interaction and renormalization corrections:
˙X=P(X) =−∇ XV(X) +N(X) +S(X),(1572)
where∇ XVdefines the metric-dependent variational descent direction on the energy landscape,N(X) aggregates
intrinsic nonlinear feedbacks and mode-coupling terms, andS(X) represents renormalization-induced corrections
arising from coarse-graining of unresolved multiscale degrees of freedom.
Equivalently, the Pellis operatorPmay be interpreted as a deformation of the pure gradient operator by a
nonlinear operator-valued perturbation:
P=−∇ XV+ ∆ nl[X],∆ nl[X]≡N(X) +S(X),(1573)
highlighting its decomposition into conservative and non-conservative contributions.
At the linearized level around a reference stateX 0, the operator admits the expansion:
P(X0 +δX) =P(X 0) +JP (X0)δX+O(∥δX∥ 2),(1574)
whereJ P =δP/δXis the Fr´ echet derivative governing local stability properties and spectral response.
In this representation, climate evolution is governed by a single effective nonlinear generator whose struc-
ture encodes energy minimization, instability amplification, and scale-dependent renormalization within a unified
dynamical framework.
Thus, the Pellis operator emerges naturally as the effective variational gradient flow operator of the climate
Lagrangian, extended by nonlinear feedback and renormalization terms that break pure gradient structure and enable
the emergence of spectral instability, bifurcation phenomena, and multistable planetary regimes.
20.4 Energy Dissipation and Lyapunov Structure
Within the Pellis variational framework, the climate energy functional acts as a natural Lyapunov candidate for
the overdamped dynamics. Define:

However, in the fully coupled climate regime, nonlinear feedback modifies the dissipation balance, and stability
is governed by the competition between gradient descent and energy injection through mode coupling. The effective
dissipation rate can be written as:
Deff (X) =∥∇ XV∥2−⟨∇ XV,N(X)⟩.(1579)
Stability is therefore guaranteed only when:

Conversely, violation of this inequality defines an energy cascade regime in which nonlinear amplification
dominates dissipation:
⟨∇XV,N(X)⟩>∥∇ XV∥2,(1581)
leading to loss of Lyapunov monotonicity, breakdown of gradient stability, and onset of runaway energy growth.
In this regime, the climate system exits the domain of classical dissipative convergence and enters a regime of
nonlinear spectral instability, where energy is continuously transferred across scales via the Pellis operator, enabling
tipping behavior and irreversible transitions between attractor basins [4,6,7,28].

Within the Pellis variational framework, stationary climate configurations are defined as critical points of the

This loss of convexity corresponds to a fundamental geometric transition in the climate action landscape:

Thus, within the Pellis framework, bifurcations are not merely dynamical phenomena but geometric transitions
of the variational structure itself, where the action functional loses convexity and the climate system reorganizes into
new classes of admissible solutions corresponding to distinct planetary regimes.

In this regime, the variational landscape develops nonlocal curvature anomalies, enabling cascades of instability

20.7 Emergent Interpretation
The variational formulation establishes that climate evolution is governed by a generalized stationary action
principle on an infinite-dimensional functional manifoldH, where admissible trajectories correspond to critical points
of the action functionalS. Within this setting, dynamical stability is equivalent to local convexity properties of the
induced energy–action landscape, as encoded by the spectral positivity of the associated second variation operator.

===== PAGE 176 =====
In particular, the climate system evolves under a constrained gradient-flow structure generated by the effective
variational derivative of the action:
˙X=−M −1(X)δS
δX + ∆nl(X),(1593)
whereMdefines the metric structure of the configuration space and ∆ nl aggregates nonlinear feedback, multiscale
coupling, and renormalization-induced corrections that deform the pure variational descent dynamics.
In this interpretation, the Pellis operator emerges as the effective generator of the reduced variational flow:
P(X)≡−M −1(X)∇XS[X] +Rnl(X),(1594)
whereR nl encapsulates the deviation from ideal gradient structure induced by nonlinear coupling, spectral deforma-
tion, and coarse-graining effects across scales.
From a geometric perspective, stability corresponds to the existence of locally convex regions in the action
landscape, characterized by positive-definite second variation:
δ2S[X]>0.(1595)
Loss of convexity therefore signals a qualitative transition in the geometry of admissible trajectories, with the
emergence of directions of negative curvature in functional space.
Within this framework, tipping points are naturally interpreted as variational saddle-node annihilations in
infinite-dimensional configuration space, where stable and unstable critical manifolds collide and disappear. Formally,
this corresponds to the coalescence of stationary solutions:
Xs→X u,(1596)
accompanied by a degeneracy of the Hessian spectrum:
∃λ∈σ(H S) such thatλ→0.(1597)
Such events mark the breakdown of stable extrema and the transition from locally confined dynamics to
globally reorganized flow regimes, where the system is no longer governed by perturbative stability around a single
attractor but instead evolves through structurally distinct planetary configurations.
Thus, planetary instability in the Pellis framework is fundamentally a geometric consequence of variational
degeneracy in functional space, where nonlinear feedback and renormalization effects drive the system toward critical
manifolds separating distinct regimes of Earth system organization [1,18,19,37,139].
21 Universal Climate Tipping Point Theorem
The Universal Climate Tipping Point Theorem formalizes the existence, characterization, and inevitability of
critical transitions in the climate system as governed by the Pellis Functional Operator. Within this framework,
tipping points are not exceptional events but structurally necessary outcomes of spectral instability in infinite-
dimensional nonlinear dynamical systems [4,6,7,9,14,18,19,28,29,37].
21.1 Preliminaries and Operator Setting
LetHbe a separable Hilbert space representing the full climate state manifold endowed with inner product⟨·,·⟩ H
and associated norm∥·∥ H. The climate evolution is formulated as a nonlinear abstract Cauchy problem onH:
d

whereP:H→His the nonlinear Pellis generator governing the coupled Earth-system dynamics.
The Pellis operator is decomposed into a linear propagation component, a nonlinear interaction term, and a
renormalization-induced structural correction:
P(X) =AX+N(X) +S(X),(1599)
whereAis a (generally unbounded) linear operator encoding baseline climate transport and dissipation mechanisms,
N(X) represents nonlinear feedback and mode coupling, andS(X) captures scale-dependent corrections arising from
coarse-graining and multilevel renormalization of unresolved degrees of freedom.
In this formulation, the linear operatorAgenerates a reference semigroupe tA describing baseline evolution
in the absence of nonlinear effects, whileNandSact as perturbations that deform both trajectory geometry and
spectral structure of the flow.

on its domain of definition, ensuring well-posedness of the evolution problem in the semigroup sense.

Within this operator setting, climate dynamics are thus interpreted as the evolution of trajectories in an
infinite-dimensional phase space driven by a nonlinear semigroup whose generator encodes transport, interaction,
and renormalization effects in a unified spectral–functional framework [15–17].
21.2 Definition of Tipping Point
Within the Pellis operator framework, a climate tipping point is defined as a finite critical timet c<∞at which
the dynamical flowX(t) = Φ t(X0) undergoes a qualitative reorganization of its invariant set structure in the state
spaceH. Formally, this corresponds to a discontinuous change in the equivalence class of trajectories under the
topology induced by invariant manifolds and attractor basins:

Thus, within the Pellis framework, a tipping point is rigorously interpreted as a spectral–topological transition
in the linearized generator of the climate flow, marking the boundary between structurally distinct dynamical regimes
in the infinite-dimensional state manifold [15–17].

21.5 Renormalization Invariance of Criticality
Within the Pellis framework, renormalization is formulated as a similarity transformation on the nonlinear gen-
eratorPacting on the infinite-dimensional climate state spaceH. LetR:H→Hdenote a coarse-graining operator
inducing a reduced-resolution representation of the system. The transformed dynamics are then governed by the
conjugated operator:
P′ =RPR−1,(1636)
which preserves the abstract dynamical structure while altering the effective scaling of spectral and nonlinear con-
tributions.
Under this transformation, the spectral measure of the operator undergoes a global rescaling induced by the
renormalization eigenvalueγ, such that:
σ(P′) =γσ(P),(1637)
whereγ∈R + encodes the effective change of dynamical scale between successive coarse-grained representations of
the climate system.
More generally, the spectral transformation can be expressed at the level of resolvent operators:
(λI−P ′)−1 =R(λI−P) −1R−1,(1638)
implying that pole structure, and hence stability information, is preserved up to renormalized scaling of spectral
parameters.
A key consequence is that criticality is invariant under renormalization flow in the sense of spectral marginality.
Specifically, if a mode lies on the stability boundary in the original representation, then its renormalized counterpart
remains marginal:
Re(λ) = 0 =⇒Re(γλ) = 0,(1639)
demonstrating that the critical manifold is invariant under multiplicative spectral scaling transformations.
This invariance implies that the set of tipping configurations forms a renormalization fixed set in operator
space:
Σc ={X∈H:α(X) = 0},R(Σ c) = Σc,(1640)
whereα(X) denotes the spectral abscissa ofP.
In this sense, climate criticality exhibits universality across scales, since the geometry of the tipping manifold
is preserved under repeated coarse-graining transformations, up to a scalar renormalization of growth rates. This
establishes that the onset of instability is not scale-dependent, but instead corresponds to a renormalization-invariant
property of the underlying nonlinear operator flow [18,19,31].

Within the spectral representation of the Pellis operator dynamics, the climate state admits an intrinsic information-

Thus, climate tipping points admit a dual interpretation as both dynamical instabilities and information-

21.7 Corollary: Inevitability of Tipping under Forcing
Within the Pellis operator framework, external forcing is introduced as an additive control term in the evolution
equation:
d

whereF(t)∈Hrepresents an externally imposed perturbation field acting on the climate state manifold.

In spectral representation, the forcing term induces continuous excitation of modes across the spectrum:

Consequently, there must exist at least one finite timet c <∞at which the stability structure of the flow is
violated, and a qualitative transition in the attractor geometry occurs:
∃tc such thatX(t) undergoes a tipping transition.(1651)
This result establishes a structural inevitability principle: sustained non-integrable forcing destroys global
spectral contractivity of the Pellis flow, ensuring that the system cannot remain indefinitely confined within a single
stability basin. In this sense, tipping is not a contingent event but an emergent consequence of persistent energy
injection into a nonlinear infinite-dimensional dynamical system [2,3,14].
21.8 Physical Interpretation
The preceding results indicate that climate tipping points should not be interpreted as rare or contingent anomalies
arising from exceptional parameter choices, but rather as structurally embedded outcomes of nonlinear spectral
evolution in forced infinite-dimensional dynamical systems. Within the Pellis formalism, the evolution of the climate
state is governed by continuous deformation of the operator spectrum under coupling, forcing, and renormalization
effects, implying that stability is generically a transient property rather than a permanent regime.
In physical terms, spectral deformation corresponds to the progressive redistribution of growth rates across
dynamically coupled modes of the Earth system. As external forcing and internal feedback mechanisms accumulate,
eigenvalues of the generatorPmigrate toward the marginal stability boundary:
Re(λ)→0,(1652)
thereby reducing the effective spectral gap and amplifying susceptibility to perturbation growth across multiple
interacting subsystems.
From this perspective, tipping behavior emerges as the macroscopic manifestation of a loss of spectral sepa-
ration between stable and unstable manifolds, leading to a collapse of predictable decoupling between fast and slow
climate components. Once this separation is sufficiently weakened, nonlinear interactions encoded inN(X) dominate
the evolution, driving coherent amplification across scales.
Accordingly, planetary instability is interpreted as the asymptotic regime of forced operator dynamics in
infinite-dimensional climate manifolds, where persistent excitation, nonlinear feedback, and renormalization effects
jointly ensure eventual departure from any initially stable configuration [6,7,9,18,37,139].
22 Comparison with Earth System Models
This section establishes a rigorous correspondence between the Universal Pellis Functional Operator framework
and state-of-the-art Earth System Models (ESMs), including coupled atmosphere–ocean general circulation models,
reduced-complexity tipping models, and stochastic climate emulators. The goal is to demonstrate that classical ESM
dynamics can be recovered as a constrained projection of the Pellis operator dynamics, while the latter extends the
predictive regime into nonlinear spectral-critical transitions not captured in standard formulations [6, 7,9, 10,23–25,
33,37,38].
22.1 Operator Embedding of Earth System Models
LetY(t)∈H ESM denote the state vector of a discretized Earth System Model (ESM), evolving under a high-
dimensional nonlinear stochastic dynamical system of the form:
d
dtY(t) =F ESM (Y(t)) +η(t),(1653)
whereF ESM :H ESM →H ESM encodes resolved physical parameterizations (atmospheric dynamics, ocean circula-
tion, cryosphere evolution, and biogeochemical feedbacks), whileη(t) represents unresolved stochastic forcing and
model error contributions.
Within the Pellis framework, we introduce a nonlinear lifting (embedding) operator
Π :H ESM→H,(1654)
which maps discretized model states into the continuum-limit Hilbert manifoldH, thereby embedding finite-resolution
Earth System dynamics into the infinite-dimensional operator-theoretic climate space:
X(t) = ΠY(t).(1655)
182

===== PAGE 183 =====
Under this embedding, the ESM dynamics are approximately conjugate to the Pellis flow, in the sense that
the lifted vector field admits the decomposition:
ΠFESM (Y)≈ AΠY+N(ΠY) +S(ΠY),(1656)
whereArepresents the linear transport–dissipation backbone,Nencodes nonlinear interaction closures, andS
accounts for renormalization-induced corrections arising from unresolved subgrid-scale processes.
The quality of this operator embedding is quantified by a projection consistency bound in operator norm:
∥ΠFESM (Y)−P(ΠY)∥ H≤ϵ proj,(1657)
whereϵ proj measures the residual mismatch between discretized model physics and the continuum Pellis generator.
In this sense,Pacts as a nonlinear spectral completion of conventional Earth System Models, extending
their finite-dimensional dynamics into a unified infinite-dimensional operator framework. The embedding Π thereby
provides a rigorous bridge between computational climate simulators and abstract spectral–variational dynamics,
allowing stability and tipping behavior to be analyzed through the spectral geometry ofPrather than the discretized
structure ofF ESM .
Consequently, tipping phenomena identified in numerical ESM simulations correspond to spectral instabilities
of the embedded Pellis flow, with projection errorsϵ proj determining the fidelity of finite-resolution approximations
to the underlying operator-level critical transitions [16–19,31].
22.2 Spectral Resolution Gap in Classical Climate Models
Classical Earth System Models operate on a discretized and numerically regularized approximation of the under-
lying climate dynamics, which induces an intrinsic truncation of the accessible spectral domain. In operator-theoretic
terms, the effective generatorF ESM acts on a finite-dimensional projection of the full phase space, resulting in a
bounded spectral support of the form:
σ(FESM )⊂{λ∈C:|λ|≤Λ c},(1658)
where Λc is an algorithmically imposed cutoff scale determined by grid resolution, time-stepping constraints, and
subgrid parameterization closures.
This truncation introduces a spectral resolution gap, whereby modes with weak growth rates and near-marginal
stability are either damped or projected out of the resolved dynamics. In particular, eigenvalues satisfying Re(λ)≈0
are systematically distorted by numerical diffusion and discretization-induced stabilization effects, effectively sup-
pressing the critical spectral region responsible for slow manifold destabilization.
In contrast, the Pellis operatorPis defined on an unbounded functional spectrum with renormalization-
consistent structure, admitting a complete decomposition into stability regimes:
σ(P) =σ stable∪σcritical∪σunstable,(1659)
where the three spectral sectors are defined by their real parts:
σstable ={λ∈σ(P) : Re(λ)<0},(1660)
σcritical ={λ∈σ(P) : Re(λ) = 0},(1661)
σunstable ={λ∈σ(P) : Re(λ)>0}.(1662)
This full spectral stratification highlights that climate tipping dynamics are fundamentally governed by transi-
tions occurring withinσ critical, where modes exhibit marginal stability and maximal sensitivity to perturbations. In
this regime, nonlinear interactions and renormalization effects determine whether trajectories are attracted toward
stability or repelled into unstable growth sectors.
The inability of classical ESMs to robustly predict tipping cascades can thus be interpreted as a consequence of
their effective suppression ofσ critical, since numerical diffusion and finite-resolution discretization tend to artificially
shift marginal modes into the stable sector:
σESM
critical−→σnum
stable.(1663)
As a result, the most dynamically significant transitions—those associated with slow amplification, critical
slowing down, and precursor signal emergence—are systematically attenuated in conventional simulations. In the
Pellis framework, however, these modes are preserved as structurally essential components of the full operator
spectrum, enabling a complete representation of tipping cascades as genuine spectral transitions in the infinite-
dimensional climate generator [4–6,28,29,32].

===== PAGE 184 =====
22.3 Tipping Dynamics Beyond Parameterized Feedbacks
In conventional Earth System Models, nonlinear feedback mechanisms are typically represented through a finite
set of prescribed parameterizations. The resulting dynamics are therefore expressed in closed form as:
dX
dt =f(X;θ),(1664)
whereθ∈R m denotes a fixed parameter vector encoding empirical closure assumptions for unresolved physical
processes. Within this structure, feedback strength and stability properties are externally imposed and remain static
throughout the evolution.
In contrast, the Pellis formulation replaces static parameterization schemes with dynamically evolving func-
tional operators acting on the full state trajectory. In this setting, parameters are no longer constants but state-
dependent functionals:
θ→Θ[X(t)],(1665)
thereby promoting the feedback structure itself into a dynamical object evolving on the climate state manifold.
The resulting evolution equation acquires a higher-order self-referential structure:
d
dtX(t) =AX+N(X) +S(X,Θ[X]),(1666)
whereAencodes linear transport and dissipation,Nrepresents intrinsic nonlinear coupling between resolved modes,
andSintroduces feedback modulation through the dynamically evolving operator Θ[X].
This construction implies that the stability landscape is no longer fixed in parameter space but co-evolves with
the system trajectory, producing a feedback loop between state evolution and effective model structure. Consequently,
the parameter manifold becomes an active dynamical subsystem governed by its own evolution equation:
dΘ
dt =R(Θ) +G(X),(1667)
whereRrepresents intrinsic renormalization flow on the parameter space andGcaptures back-reaction from the
evolving climate state.
This coupled system defines a hybrid dynamical hierarchy in which state variables and effective parameters are
mutually dependent, generating a self-referential instability structure absent from conventional Earth System Model
architectures. In particular, feedback strength is no longer externally prescribed but emerges endogenously through
operator evolution, allowing for spontaneous amplification of instability channels when the coupled flow approaches
critical regimes.
Within this framework, tipping behavior can be interpreted as a joint state–parameter bifurcation, where both
X(t) and Θ[X(t)] simultaneously undergo qualitative reorganization under the coupled action ofPandR, leading
to structurally novel regimes of Earth system dynamics [14,18,19,27,30,33].
22.4 Error Growth and Predictability Horizon
In conventional Earth System Model (ESM) formulations, predictability is typically characterized by the growth
of infinitesimal perturbationsδY(t) along a reference trajectory, governed asymptotically by the maximal Lyapunov
exponent:
∥δY(t)∥∼e λmaxt,(1668)
whereλ max is computed from the linearized tangent dynamics of the discretized model flow. This scalar characteri-
zation implicitly assumes a dominant instability direction and a finite-dimensional effective error growth mechanism.
In contrast, the Pellis formulation embeds perturbation dynamics into the full spectral geometry of the non-
linear operatorP. Infinitesimal deviations evolve as distributed spectral superpositions across the entire operator
spectrum:
δX(t) =
Z
σ(P)
δa(λ,0)e λtϕλdµ(λ),(1669)
where each spectral mode contributes a distinct exponential growth or decay rate, resulting in a continuum of
instability scales rather than a single dominant exponent.
Within this framework, predictability is governed by a time-dependent spectral Lyapunov functional rather
than a scalar exponent. We define the effective growth rate of perturbation energy as:
Λeff (t) =
Z
σ(P)
Re(λ)|a(λ,t)| 2dµ(λ),(1670)

which encodes the instantaneous redistribution of perturbation energy across stable, marginal, and unstable spectral
subspaces.
The evolution of Λ eff (t) reflects nonlinear transfer of perturbation energy between spectral sectors induced
byN(X) and renormalization effectsS(X), leading to a cascade-like amplification mechanism rather than uniform
exponential divergence.
Consequently, loss of predictability in Pellis dynamics is not governed by a single exponential timescale but
emerges from a multiscale spectral cascade instability, in which initially localized perturbations progressively spread
across the operator spectrum:
suppδa(λ,t)−→σ(P).(1671)
This mechanism implies that predictability horizons are determined by the collective behavior of interact-
ing spectral bands rather than a single dominant Lyapunov exponent. As the system approaches criticality, the
density of near-marginal modes increases, effectively flattening the spectral stability landscape and accelerating the
redistribution of perturbation energy.
Therefore, within the Pellis framework, predictability breakdown is fundamentally a spectral cascade phe-
nomenon rather than a one-dimensional exponential divergence, consistent with multiscale instability propagation
and operator-driven error amplification across the full climate state manifold [7–9,28,29].
22.5 Recovery of Classical Climate Models as Linear Limits
Classical climate dynamics emerge as a controlled asymptotic regime of the full Pellis operator when nonlinear
feedback amplification and renormalization-driven corrections become subdominant relative to the linear generator.
Formally, this corresponds to the scaling limit:
∥N(X)∥≪∥AX∥,∥S(X)∥≪1,(1672)
which defines a perturbative contraction of the full functional dynamics onto its linear tangent structure.
Under this separation of scales, the evolution operator admits the asymptotic expansion:
P(X) =AX+ϵN(X) +δS(X), ϵ,δ→0,(1673)
so that higher-order functional corrections vanish in the renormalization limit and the flow becomes effectively linear.
Consequently, the governing dynamics reduce to:
P(X)→AX,(1674)
yielding a closed linear response system in which the evolution is fully determined by the spectrum ofA:
X(t) =eAtX0,(1675)
with stability entirely governed byσ(A) and perturbation statistics constrained to Gaussian or near-Gaussian regimes
under central-limit-like aggregation of unresolved degrees of freedom.
In this limit, the climate system behaves as a linear dissipative operator flow onH, and all nonlinear cascade
mechanisms—mode coupling, spectral transfer, and renormalization feedback—collapse into effective eddy-diffusive
closures. The resulting structure coincides with the standard formulation of linear response Earth System Models,
in which perturbations evolve independently and superposition holds globally.
Therefore, classical Earth System Models are rigorously interpreted as first-order truncations of the full Pellis
operator hierarchy, obtained by neglecting both nonlinear spectral transfer and scale-dependent renormalization
feedback:
P=A+O(N,S).(1676)
This establishes a precise operator-theoretic embedding in which conventional climate modeling frameworks
arise as linearized projections of a higher-order nonlinear spectral dynamics, valid only away from critical regimes
whereNandSbecome dynamically relevant [23–25,30,31].
22.6 Conclusion: Structural Superiority of the Pellis Framework
The comparative analysis establishes that conventional Earth System Models correspond to a restricted finite-
resolution projection of an underlying infinite-dimensional nonlinear spectral geometry. In this sense, their predictive
structure is not fundamentally incorrect but structurally incomplete, as essential components of the full operator
dynamics are systematically eliminated through truncation, parameter closure, and numerical regularization.
The Pellis framework extends this reduced description into a fully coupled spectral–functional theory by
incorporating three non-perturbative structural layers:
185

===== PAGE 186 =====
(i) full spectral decomposition,
(ii) nonlinear renormalization coupling,
(iii) functional feedback closure.
Each of these components contributes an independent source of dynamical complexity. The spectral decompo-
sition resolves the continuum of stability modes; renormalization coupling governs scale-dependent reorganization of
effective dynamics; and functional feedback closure enforces self-consistency of the operator evolution on the climate
state manifold.
Within this unified structure, climate tipping points are no longer interpreted as externally triggered threshold
crossings in reduced parameter space. Instead, they emerge as intrinsic phase transitions of the global operator field,
defined directly in terms of spectral topology:
P:H→H,0∈Spec(P) =⇒loss of global stability / planetary instability.(1677)
This condition reflects the coalescence of marginal modes at the origin of the complex spectral plane, signaling
a collapse of the stability gap and the emergence of long-range dynamical coupling across previously decoupled
subsystems. In this regime, perturbations cannot be localized to individual components but instead propagate
through the full spectral manifold, producing system-wide reconfiguration of attractor structure.
Accordingly, planetary instability is interpreted as a universal spectral phase transition governed by the geome-
try ofσ(P), rather than a sequence of independent subsystem bifurcations. This formulation unifies renormalization
theory, nonlinear dynamical systems, and Earth system physics within a single operator-theoretic framework, in
agreement with observed multiscale cascade behavior and emergent critical transitions in coupled climate dynam-
ics [1,4,6,10,18,19,27,33,37,139].
23 Testable Predictions and Observable Signatures
A central requirement for the Universal Pellis Functional Operator framework is falsifiability through measurable,
multi-scale observables. This section derives a hierarchy of testable predictions that distinguish Pellis dynamics
from classical Earth System Model outputs and standard stochastic climate theories. The predictions are formulated
as spectral, statistical, and variational signatures directly observable in climate reanalysis datasets, paleoclimate
reconstructions, and high-resolution coupled model outputs [6,7,9,10,28,29,33,35,37,38].

23.6 Topological Signature of Phase Transition Manifolds
A central prediction of the Pellis framework is that climate tipping points correspond not only to spectral insta-
bilities of the operatorP(t), but also to topological bifurcations in the geometry of its associated invariant spectral
manifolds. LetM λ(t) denote the spectral manifold defined by level sets of the resolvent or spectral projection struc-
ture ofP(t), embedded in an appropriate functional phase space. The topology of these manifolds is characterized
by invariants such as the Euler characteristic:
χ(Mλ(t))̸= constant,(1721)
indicating that the system undergoes a genuine topological phase transition rather than a purely smooth deformation
of dynamical trajectories.
More generally, let{M λ(t)}λ∈σ(P) denote the foliation of phase space induced by spectral decomposition.
Then tipping events correspond to non-homotopic transitions between foliation classes, i.e.
Mλ(t−)̸≃M λ(t+),(1722)
where≃denotes homotopy equivalence. This implies that the system crosses a topological bifurcation boundary in
the space of invariant spectral geometries.
From a dynamical systems perspective, this breakdown of topological equivalence manifests as a failure of
structural stability in the reduced-order representation of the climate state. Let Φ :H→R m denote a nonlinear
embedding (e.g., a spectral manifold learning map or projection onto dominant empirical modes). Then near a
critical transition timet c, the embedding loses continuity in its incremental evolution:

23.7 Prediction Summary and Falsifiability Structure
The full set of observable predictions generated by the Pellis framework admits a hierarchical organization of
coupled spectral, probabilistic, energetic, and topological diagnostics. Each layer corresponds to a distinct projection
of the underlying non-normal operator dynamics ofP(t), and jointly defines a multi-modal signature of approaching
criticality:
(P1) Re(λ)→0 spectral drift,(1726)
(P2)τ eff→∞distributed slowing down,(1727)
(P3)κ n↑non-Gaussian amplification,(1728)
(P4) SAI(t)→∞,(1729)
(P5)E(k,t) scaling instability,(1730)
(P6)χ(M) topology change.(1731)
Importantly, these signatures are not independent observables but arise from a shared underlying spectral
measure deformationµ t(λ) and a common amplitude fielda(λ,t) associated with the evolving climate operatorP(t).
Consistency of the framework therefore imposes cross-correlation constraints of the form:
(P1)⇒(P2)⇒(P3)⇒(P4)⇒(P5)⇒(P6),(1732)
in the asymptotic approach to the critical manifoldt→t −
c , while violations of this ordering indicate either regime
heterogeneity or breakdown of the spectral closure assumption.
From a statistical physics perspective, each prediction corresponds to a different moment or functional of the
spectral energy distribution:
µt(λ) =|a(λ,t)| 2dµ(λ),(1733)
where (P1) governs support drift, (P2) governs resolvent scaling, (P3) governs higher-order moment divergence, (P4)
governs signed measure imbalance, (P5) governs scale-dependent flux redistribution, and (P6) governs geometric
invariants of induced phase-space manifolds.
The empirical falsifiability structure is therefore inherently multi-layered: each prediction can be independently
tested, but the theory is only fully validated if all six signatures co-emerge in a temporally coherent pre-critical
window. This imposes a strong constraint on observational consistency across heterogeneous datasets, including
reanalysis climate fields, satellite-derived energy budgets, and paleo-climatic proxy reconstructions [23–25,33,37,38,
139].
In particular, the Pellis framework predicts a synchronized onset of anomalies across spectral, statistical,
energetic, and topological observables, implying that early-warning signals should not appear in isolation but as a
correlated phase-transition cluster in diagnostic space.
The central empirical implication is therefore that climate tipping points are not isolated bifurcations in
low-dimensional reduced models, but coordinated spectral reorganizations of a high-dimensional nonlinear operator
field:
Tipping≡Spectral Phase Reorganization ofP .(1734)
24 Predictions of the Theory
The Universal Pellis Function Theory yields a closed hierarchy of quantitative predictions that unify spectral insta-
bility, renormalization flow, and multiscale climate tipping behavior into a single falsifiable mathematical structure.
In contrast to empirical parameterizations in classical climate science, all predictions emerge directly from the spectral
geometry of the nonlinear operatorPacting on the climate state spaceH[6,7,9,10,18,19,23,28,29,33,37,38,139].

24.5 Prediction of Irreversibility Hysteresis Loops
A defining structural consequence of the Pellis framework is the emergence of irreversible hysteresis in the effective
functional state space of the climate system. This irreversibility is not imposed phenomenologically but arises from
non-normal spectral deformation, path-dependent operator evolution, and loss of dynamical invertibility near the
critical manifold.
Let Φt denote the nonlinear flow generated by the time-dependent Pellis operatorP(t) on the state spaceH.

Pellis regime, operator non-normality and spectral collapse induce a breakdown of invertibility, such that forward
and backward flows evolve on distinct effective manifolds in phase space.

is consistent with observed hysteresis phenomena across multiple components of the Earth system, including ocean
circulation, ice-sheet dynamics, and atmospheric circulation regimes [4,5,8,9,33].

24.6 Universal Climate Instability Functional Law
The Pellis framework culminates in a single scalar diagnostic functional that encodes the net stability balance of
the entire planetary climate operator field. Unlike traditional indicators based on isolated eigenvalues or reduced-
order proxies, this quantity aggregates the full spectral energy distribution weighted by dynamical growth rates. Let
P(t) denote the evolving climate operator with spectral measureµ(λ) and modal amplitudesa(λ,t). The Universal
Climate Instability Functional is defined as:
U(t) =
Z
σ(P(t))
Re(λ)|a(λ,t)| 2dµ(λ).(1770)
This functional can be interpreted as the net spectral energy flux across the stability boundary, where positive
contributions correspond to unstable or weakly damped modes and negative contributions correspond to dissipative
stabilizing modes. Equivalently, it represents the first spectral moment of the stability distribution:
U(t) =E µt

Re(λ)


whereµ t(λ) =|a(λ,t)| 2dµ(λ) is the time-dependent spectral energy measure.
In this formulation, planetary stability is governed not by isolated eigenvalue crossings but by a global balance
condition between stabilizing and destabilizing spectral mass. The universal instability criterion is therefore expressed
as:
U(t)≥0⇒planetary-scale instability onset.(1772)
More precisely, the transition corresponds to a sign inversion of the spectral stability functional:
U(t)<0 (globally dissipative regime),U(t)≥0 (instability-dominated regime).(1773)
Near criticality, the functionalU(t) undergoes a nonlinear amplification due to spectral condensation near
Re(λ) = 0. In this regime, the measureµ t becomes increasingly concentrated on marginal modes, yielding a reduced
effective stability margin:
µt(λ)⇀µ c(λ),supp(µ c)⊂{λ: Re(λ) = 0}.(1774)
Consequently, the instability transition can be characterized by a collapse of spectral balance rather than a
discrete bifurcation, with the asymptotic scaling behavior:
U(t)∼(t c−t)−γ, γ >0,(1775)
indicating divergence of instability pressure as the system approaches the critical manifold.
From an operator-theoretic perspective,U(t) acts as a Lyapunov-like functional for the non-normal evolution
generated byP(t), but generalized to incorporate spectral weight redistribution and non-equilibrium energy transfer
across modes.
Thus, the complexity of Earth system risk assessment reduces to the monitoring of a single spectral functional
threshold, while preserving full multiscale dynamical richness through its origin in the underlying operator geometry:
U(t) encodes global stability ofP(t),(1776)
providing a unified criterion for planetary-scale instability across coupled climate subsystems [1,6,10,18,19,37,38,139].

The complete predictive structure of the Pellis framework can be organized into a coherent hierarchy of coupled
spectral, temporal, dynamical, and geometric signatures. Each prediction corresponds to a distinct projection of
the evolving operator fieldP(t) and its induced spectral measureµ t(λ), while remaining mutually constrained by a
shared underlying critical manifold structure.
(P1)σ(P)→σ c,(1777)
(P2)τ eff∼|t c−t|−γ,(1778)
(P3) tipping synchronization across subsystems,(1779)
(P4) nonlinear amplification of weak forcing,(1780)
(P5) irreversible hysteresis emergence,(1781)
(P6)U(t)≥0 universal instability threshold.(1782)

Crucially, these predictions are not independent empirical statements but arise from a single underlying
spectral phase transition in the operator-valued climate dynamics. In particular, they correspond to distinct limiting
behaviors of the same spectral energy measure:
µt(λ) =|a(λ,t)| 2dµ(λ),(1783)
with (P1) governing support collapse, (P2) governing inverse damping divergence, (P3) governing cross-operator
spectral alignment, (P4) governing nonlinear renormalization of perturbation response, (P5) governing breakdown
of invertibility in phase-space flows, and (P6) governing global sign transition of spectral stability balance.
The theory therefore imposes strong consistency constraints across observables: near the critical regimet→t −
c ,
all six signatures must emerge in a temporally correlated and structurally coherent manner. Failure of joint emergence
would indicate either violation of spectral closure assumptions or decomposition breakdown of the effective operator
representation of the climate system.
This leads to a unified falsifiability structure in which empirical validation requires simultaneous detection of
spectral compression, timescale divergence, cross-system synchronization, nonlinear amplification, hysteresis forma-
tion, and instability functional sign change within a shared pre-critical temporal window.
Together, these results establish a unified, experimentally testable framework in which climate tipping behavior
is interpreted as a global spectral phase transition of a nonlinear operator field governing Earth system dynamics
[6,7,9,10,23,27,33,37,38,139].
25 Experimental Validation and Falsifiable Predictions
A physically meaningful theoretical framework for climate criticality must ultimately be reducible to empirical
verification. In the Universal Pellis Function Theory, validation is formulated as a spectral-consistency problem
between observed climate observables and the inferred eigenstructure of the nonlinear operatorP. This section
establishes a rigorous mapping between measurable datasets and operator-theoretic predictions, enabling direct
falsifiability under real-world Earth system observations [6,7,9,10,23,28,29,33,35,37,38,139].

Lyapunov spectra providing a direct and falsifiable diagnostic of universality in climate criticality [7–9,28,29].

25.4 Non-Gaussian Transition Diagnostics
A central diagnostic implication of the Pellis framework is that proximity to critical transitions induces a sys-
tematic breakdown of Gaussian closure in climate observables. LetX(t) denote a generic scalar climate variable
extracted from the projection of the state vectorX obs(t). In the Gaussian regime, all higher-order cumulants beyond
second order vanish; however, near spectral criticality this structure is generically violated due to nonlinear spectral
condensation and amplification of rare-event modes.
The fourth-order cumulant (kurtosis excess) is given by:
