condition as the spectral measureµ t(λ) concentrates near weakly damped and unstable modes.

purely stochastic correction. As spectral condensation intensifies, the ratio of higher-order cumulants to second-order
moments exhibits divergent scaling:

This behavior is consistent with the transition from central-limit-type statistics to L´ evy-like or intermittency-
dominated distributions driven by non-normal amplification of weak spectral modes. In particular, skewness and
kurtosis escalate as effective probability mass concentrates in rare-event-generating regions of the spectrum.
From an operator-theoretic perspective, this corresponds to a deformation of the induced invariant measure:

These predictions are directly testable using reanalysis datasets, extreme-event statistics, and paleoclimate
proxy reconstructions, where systematic increases in kurtosis and higher-order cumulants should precede tipping
transitions in key Earth system components [4,5,27,33].

25.7 Paleo-Climate Reconstruction Consistency
Paleo-climatic archives provide a uniquely long-timescale observational window for testing the spectral stability
structure predicted by the Pellis framework. Ice cores, sediment records, speleothems, and marine proxies encode
integrated responses of the Earth system to past perturbations, enabling reconstruction of an effective historical
operatorP paleo(t) governing coarse-grained climate evolution over millennial to glacial-interglacial scales.
Within this formulation, paleo-reconstructed dynamics are interpreted as projections of the underlying spectral
operator onto a temporally filtered observational subspace. The key consistency condition is spectral equivalence (in
a weak convergence sense) between reconstructed and theoretical operators:

25.8 Unified Experimental Falsification Criterion
A defining feature of the Pellis framework is that it is not constructed as a purely descriptive or phenomenolog-
ical model, but as a tightly constrained operator-theoretic hypothesis with multiple independent, cross-validating
empirical failure modes. In this sense, the theory is strongly falsifiable through simultaneous violation of spectral,
temporal, statistical, and geometric signatures.
Formally, letH obs denote the full set of observational diagnostics derived from climate data assimilation,
including spectral densities, autocorrelation structure, cumulant hierarchies, and reconstructed operator estimates.
The theory is rejected if any of the following independent falsification conditions hold:
(F1) ∆ σ =
Z

(F2)τ obs

(F3) SAI(t) remains uniformly bounded for allt,(1834)
(F4)κ n(X) fails to exhibit non-Gaussian scaling growth,(1835)
(F5)σ(P(t)) shows no evidence of spectral collapse or condensation.(1836)
Each falsification channel corresponds to a distinct structural aspect of the underlying operator dynamics.
Condition (F1) tests spectral identifiability, (F2) tests temporal memory divergence, (F3) tests instability imbalance
growth, (F4) tests higher-order statistical deformation, and (F5) tests global spectral phase transition behavior.
25.9 Conclusion: Empirical Closure of the Theory
The cumulative experimental and theoretical structure developed throughout the Pellis framework demonstrates
that climate tipping behavior is not most naturally understood as a collection of isolated nonlinear bifurcations, but
rather as the emergent manifestation of a single underlying operator reconstruction problem. In this formulation, the
Earth system is represented as an evolving spectral object whose observable dynamics encode incomplete projections
of a higher-dimensional governing operatorP(t).
The empirical objective of climate science within this framework is therefore reinterpreted as the inverse
spectral identification problem:
Rd
 
Xobs(t)

−→ P(t),(1837)
where observational data are used to reconstruct both the spectrum and the induced spectral measure of the under-
lying climate operator.
This leads to a compact formal equivalence between dynamical evolution and spectral reconstruction:
Climate Dynamics≡Spectral Reconstruction ofP(t) from Data ,(1838)
which expresses the central closure principle of the theory: all macroscopic climate phenomena correspond to iden-
tifiable transformations in the spectral geometry of a single nonlinear operator field.
Within this interpretation, tipping points are not additional dynamical structures imposed on the system,
but are instead emergent singularities in the reconstruction process itself, characterized by spectral condensation,
resolvent divergence, and collapse of stability partitioning.
The framework therefore establishes a closed theoretical loop between
(i) forward operator dynamics,
(ii) observational projection, and
(iii) inverse spectral reconstruction, ensuring that prediction and validation are governed by a single consistent
mathematical structure.
This closure transforms climate criticality into a fully testable spectral inference problem grounded in operator
theory and functional analysis [1,6,7,10,23,27,33,37,38,139].
26 Applications and Practical Implications
This section develops the operational consequences of the Universal Pellis Function Theory of Climate Criticality,
focusing on how the abstract spectral–functional framework translates into measurable, predictive, and intervention-
relevant structures in real Earth system dynamics. In contrast to purely descriptive climate models, the Pellis
framework provides a mathematically rigorous mapping between operator-level instability and physically observable
regime transitions [1,4,6,18,23,33].
26.1 From Abstract Operators to Physical Observables
Within the Pellis framework, the climate system is formalized as an evolution problem on a high-dimensional (in
general infinite-dimensional) Hilbert spaceHof physically admissible climate states. The universal governing object

In this setting, physically measurable climate variables are not fundamental state coordinates but projection
functionals of the underlying dynamical field. LetO i(t) denote an observable (e.g., global mean temperatureT(t),
ocean salinityS(t), or ice-sheet mass balanceI(t)). These are defined via bounded linear functionals onH:

whereψ i∈Hare observational kernels representing measurement operators induced by satellite retrievals, in situ
sampling networks, or proxy reconstructions.
More generally, the full observational vectorO(t) can be expressed as a compact operator projection:

whereM:H→R m is the measurement operator defining the observational subspace of the climate system.
Under spectral decomposition ofP(t), the observable dynamics inherit a modal expansion of the form:

underlying spectral dynamics.
Instability in the observable sector arises when the spectral support ofP(t) intersects the unstable half-plane.
In particular, the existence of any mode satisfying

Thus, classical tipping-point behavior emerges as a low-dimensional projection of a high-dimensional spectral

of eigenvalues across the stability boundary:
Re(λ)>0⇒observable regime transition or nonlinear saturation.(1846)
This establishes a direct and testable mapping between abstract operator dynamics and measurable climate
variables, ensuring that all predicted instability phenomena are, in principle, empirically accessible through appro-
priately chosen observational kernels [4,6,28,29].

26.3 Control-Theoretic Interpretation of Climate Intervention
Within the Pellis framework, climate intervention is naturally formulated as a distributed control problem on
an infinite-dimensional dynamical system. External forcing is represented as a control inputu(t) acting through a

indicating emergent coherence in the approach to global criticality.
This structure provides a direct mathematical link between AMOC collapse, ice-sheet instability, and monsoon
transitions as manifestations of a unified spectral instability cascade in the coupled operatorP[11–13,15,32,35].
26.6 Policy-Relevant Interpretation of Tipping Risk
Within the Pellis framework, policy-relevant risk assessment is formulated as a cumulative hazard functional over
the trajectory of spectral instability. Rather than treating tipping risk as an instantaneous probability, the system
is modeled as a time-accumulating exposure to positive spectral instability, as encoded by the Computable Climate
Stability Index (CSI). The tipping probability functional is defined as:
Ptip(T) = 1−exp

This formulation embeds tipping risk within a nonhomogeneous Poisson-like survival structure, where the
instantaneous hazard function is given by:
h(t) = max(0,CSI(t)).(1878)
Accordingly, the survival probability of remaining within the stable basin up to timeTis:

A key implication is that tipping risk is path-dependent and accelerative: once CSI(t) becomes positive, the
hazard functional enters a self-reinforcing regime in which spectral drift amplifies future instability accumulation:
d

This nonlinear feedback structure implies that marginal increases in spectral instability have disproportionate
effects on long-horizon tipping probabilities, particularly near the critical manifold where CSI(t)≈0 +. In this regime,
risk becomes dominated by rare spectral excursions rather than mean dynamics.
From a policy perspective, the formulation provides a continuously updated, data-assimilative risk metric in
which mitigation strategies correspond to interventions that enforce CSI(t)<0 over sustained temporal intervals,
thereby suppressing the integrated hazard functional and restoring exponential stability of the survival probability.
26.7 Summary of Practical Consequences
The Universal Pellis Function Theory implies a fundamental reformulation of climate science in which predictabil-
ity, controllability, and risk quantification are no longer governed primarily by perturbative expansions around
equilibria, but instead by the global spectral geometry of the governing operator fieldP(t). In this view, the Earth
system is best understood as a continuously deforming spectral manifold whose stability properties are encoded in
the distribution, drift, and condensation of eigenvalues rather than in local state-space trajectories alone.
Consequently, classical approaches based on linear response theory, finite-dimensional closure assumptions, or
weakly nonlinear approximations are recovered only as low-order projections of a more general operator-theoretic
structure. The true organizing principle is the evolution of the spectral measure:
µt(λ) =|a(λ,t)| 2dµ(λ),(1882)
whose redistribution across the stability boundary Re(λ) = 0 determines emergent regime transitions.

Within this framework, climate predictability is explicitly tied to spectral gap persistence:
τpred(t)∼ 1
∆λmin(t),(1883)
while loss of predictability corresponds to spectral collapse, i.e. ∆λmin(t)→0. Control feasibility, in turn, is governed
by operator conditioning, with intervention cost diverging as the system approaches criticality. Risk is unified through
integrated spectral hazard functionals such as CSI(t) andP tip(T), which encode instability accumulation over time
rather than instantaneous deviations.
This synthesis reframes climate dynamics as a problem of nonlinear spectral stability theory in infinite-
dimensional spaces, where tipping points correspond to functional phase transitions in the operator spectrum and
emergent universality classes arise from shared spectral collapse mechanisms across subsystems.
In summary, the framework replaces traditional reductionist climate modeling paradigms with a unified
operator-theoretic structure in which all major Earth system behaviors—prediction, instability, synchronization,
and control—are manifestations of evolving spectral geometry in a high-dimensional dynamical system [1,4,6,18,23,
33,37,139].
27 Limitations and Model Assumptions
While the Universal Pellis Function Theory of Climate Criticality provides a unified spectral–functional framework
for describing tipping points and planetary instability, its mathematical structure is necessarily built upon a hierarchy
of idealizations. These assumptions enable a tractable operator-theoretic representation of the Earth system, but
they simultaneously introduce constraints on direct empirical identifiability, spectral reconstruction stability, and
rigorous inversion in strongly nonlinear regimes [1,2,16–18,23,33].
27.1 Hilbert Space Idealization of the Climate State
The theory assumes that the full climate state can be embedded in a separable Hilbert spaceHequipped with
inner product structure:
X(t)∈H,⟨X 1,X 2⟩H<∞.(1884)
This assumption encodes square-integrability and finite-energy constraints across all resolved climate fields.
However, real Earth system observables may exhibit singular components, discontinuities, and measure-valued con-
tributions that violate Hilbert regularity. In such cases, the appropriate state space may extend to a distributional
or Banach setting:
X(t)∈D ′(Ω) or more generallyX(t)∈B(Ω),(1885)
implying that the spectral decomposition ofPmust be reformulated in terms of generalized eigenfunctionals rather
than classical eigenmodes.
Consequently, the spectral measureµ(λ) may fail to admit a purely absolutely continuous representation:
µ(λ) =µ ac(λ) +µsing(λ),(1886)
introducing singular spectral components associated with abrupt climate transitions and non-smooth dynamical
events.
27.2 Smoothness and Differentiability of the Pellis Operator
The governing evolution equation assumes a sufficiently smooth operator field:
d
dtX(t) =P(X(t)),(1887)
withPtypically assumed to be at least Fr´ echet differentiable onH:
P∈C 1(H).(1888)
However, near tipping thresholds, climate dynamics often exhibit nonsmooth bifurcations, discontinuous regime
switches, and set-valued evolution. In such cases, the Gˆ ateaux derivative may fail to exist:
lim
ϵ→0
P(X+ϵY)−P(X)
ϵ ̸∃,(1889)
implying that classical linearization and local spectral perturbation theory lose validity.
This breakdown introduces the need for generalized differential inclusions:
d
dtX(t)∈P(X(t)),(1890)
which replace deterministic flows with multivalued dynamical evolutions in non-smooth regimes.

27.3 Spectral Completeness Assumption
The spectral representation of the state is assumed to take the form:
X(t) =
Z
σ(P)
a(λ,t)ϕλdµ(λ),(1891)
requiring completeness of generalized eigenfunctions and the existence of a stable spectral resolution.
In strongly non-normal or nonlinear regimes, this assumption may fail due to spectral instability and pseu-
dospectral inflation. In particular, the operator spectrum may not decompose cleanly into point and continuous
parts:
σ(P)̸=σ p(P)∪σ c(P),(1892)
and transient growth may dominate asymptotic eigenvalue structure.
In such regimes, pseudospectral sets become dynamically relevant:
σϵ(P) ={λ∈C:∥(P−λI) −1∥>ϵ −1},(1893)
implying that apparent stability based on eigenvalues alone may be misleading.
27.4 Closure Problem in Multiscale Coupling
The decomposition
P=A+N+S(1894)
implicitly assumes closure of unresolved subgrid processes and multiscale feedbacks.
However, scale interactions generate an infinite renormalization hierarchy:
Sn+1 =F(S n),(1895)
implying that no finite truncation is exact. The effective operator at resolution levelNtherefore satisfies:
P(N) =P−R (N),(1896)
whereR (N) represents unresolved renormalization residue terms.
Exact recovery of the full operator requires the limit:
lim

which is generally unattainable in practical computational frameworks.
27.5 Assumption of Stationary Spectral Structure
Many derivations rely on quasi-stationary spectral structure:

allowing eigenvalues and spectral measures to be treated as slowly varying.
In strongly forced regimes, however, the operator becomes explicitly time-dependent:
P=P(t),(1899)
leading to non-autonomous spectral transport governed by:
dλ(t)

which invalidates stationary bifurcation analysis and introduces history-dependent spectral drift.
27.6 Neglect of External Stochastic Forcing Structure
In its deterministic formulation, the Pellis operator evolution excludes stochastic forcing:
d
dtX(t) =P(X(t)).(1901)

whereW t is a cylindrical Wiener process onH.

27.7 Parameter Identifiability and Observability Constraints
The inverse mapping from observations to operator structure:
X(t)7→P(1904)
is generally non-injective and ill-posed.
Distinct operators may generate observationally indistinguishable dynamics under finite measurement resolu-

implying structural non-identifiability and inverse spectral ambiguity.
This leads to a fundamental identifiability constraint governed by an information-theoretic bound:

27.8 Summary of Theoretical Constraints
Overall, the Pellis framework should be interpreted as a universal organizing principle for spectral climate dynamics
rather than a fully closed predictive simulator. Its validity is strongest in regimes where:
∥N∥∼∥A∥,and∥S∥≪∥A∥,(1907)
corresponding to near-critical but still resolvable dynamical balance between linear instability and nonlinear satura-
tion. Beyond these regimes—particularly in strongly stochastic, discontinuous, or deeply non-normal settings—the
theory must be augmented by stochastic analysis, nonsmooth dynamical systems theory, and measure-theoretic
operator extensions to preserve predictive consistency [1,4,6,18,23,33,37,139].
28 Future Work
The Universal Pellis Function Theory of Climate Criticality establishes a spectral–functional foundation for
understanding planetary instability, yet its full mathematical, computational, and empirical completion remains an
open research program. Future developments must systematically extend the operator framework beyond idealized
regimes by incorporating stochasticity, strong nonlinearity, adaptive coupling, and data-driven reconstruction of
spectral structure in high-dimensional Earth system dynamics [1,2,6,18,23,33,37,139].
28.1 Rigorous Functional-Analytic Extension of the Pellis Operator
A first foundational direction is the embedding of the Pellis operator into a rigorously defined functional-analytic
class. In full generality, one seeks:
P:D(P)⊂X→X,(1908)
whereXextends beyond Hilbert structure to Banach, Sobolev, or measure-valued state spaces depending on the
regularity class of climate observables.
A central open problem is the characterization ofPas a generator of a strongly continuous semigroup:

together with the infinitesimal generator identity:

which determine whether the global flow admits attractor structure or finite-time blow-up.
28.2 Nonlinear Spectral Theory and Generalized Eigenstructures
A key extension concerns nonlinear spectral theory, where the operator is no longer linearizable globally:
P(X)̸=LX.(1912)
Future work must define generalized nonlinear eigenmodes satisfying a residual-balanced eigenvalue relation:
P(ϕλ) =λϕ λ +R(ϕλ),(1913)

28.8 Final Perspective
Ultimately, the future of the Universal Pellis Function Theory lies in the synthesis of spectral geometry, stochastic

29 Discussion
The Universal Pellis Function Theory of Climate Criticality proposes a unified operator-theoretic perspective in
which climate dynamics are governed not merely by differential equations on physical fields, but by the evolving
spectral geometry of a nonlinear operator fieldP. Within this formulation, the Earth system is elevated from a
trajectory-based dynamical system to a spectral object whose stability is encoded in the structure, deformation, and
collapse ofσ(P). This provides a natural extension of classical dynamical systems theory, bifurcation theory, and
statistical physics into a single functional-analytic framework [1,2,4,6,18,22,23,33,37,139].
29.1 Conceptual Shift from State-Space to Operator-Space Dynamics
Traditional Earth system modeling is formulated in state space:
d
dtX(t) =F(X(t)),(1936)
whereFis a nonlinear vector field on a finite- or infinite-dimensional phase space.
215

===== PAGE 216 =====
The Pellis formulation replaces this structure with an operator-valued flow:
d
dtX(t) =P(X(t)),(1937)
wherePacts as a nonlinear evolution generator whose properties depend on the instantaneous state of the system.
The fundamental object of analysis becomes the spectral measure:
σ(P) ={λ∈C:∃ϕ̸= 0,Pϕ=λϕ},(1938)
with climate evolution interpreted as a continuous deformation of this spectral set:
t7→σ(P(t)).(1939)
In this sense, instability is no longer a trajectory divergence in phase space but a geometric instability of
spectral support in the complex plane.
29.2 Unification of Bifurcation Theory and Spectral Instability
Classical bifurcation theory characterizes transitions through parameter-dependent equilibria:
F(X;µ) = 0.(1940)
In the Pellis framework, bifurcations are equivalently characterized by spectral crossings:
Re(λ(µ)) = 0, λ(µ)∈σ(P µ).(1941)
This defines a critical manifold in operator space:
T={µ∈R:∃λ∈σ(P µ),Re(λ) = 0}.(1942)
Higher-order degeneracies correspond to spectral coalescence:
λi(µc) =λ j(µc),∇ µλi(µc)≈0,(1943)
which generalizes classical saddle-node, Hopf, and transcritical bifurcations into a unified spectral geometry classifi-
cation.
29.3 Interpretation of Climate Tipping Points as Functional Phase Transitions
A central conceptual result is the identification of tipping points with non-analytic transformations of spectral
measures: d
dµρ(λ;µ)/∈C 0,(1944)
whereρ(λ;µ) denotes the spectral density function.
This structure is directly analogous to thermodynamic phase transitions, where non-analyticity emerges in
free energy functionals:
F(µ) =− 1

Criticality is therefore associated with divergence of response functionals:

establishing a direct correspondence between spectral instability and macroscopic susceptibility blow-up.
29.4 Relation to Renormalization and Universality
The renormalization structure of the Pellis operator is expressed as:
RP=γP,(1947)
suggesting scale invariance under coarse-graining transformations in operator space.
This induces a renormalization flow:
Pn+1 =F(P n),(1948)
with fixed points satisfying:
P∗ =F(P ∗).(1949)
Linear stability of these fixed points is governed by:
ρ(DF(P∗))<1,(1950)
defining universality classes of climate dynamics characterized by invariant spectral scaling exponents.

29.5 Interplay Between Determinism and Effective Stochasticity
Although the fundamental formulation is deterministic:
d

effective stochasticity emerges through unresolved multiscale interactions.
This leads to a decomposition:

whereη unresolved acts as an induced stochastic forcing term.
In the continuum limit, this yields a stochastic evolution equation:
dX(t) =P(X(t))dt+ Σ(X(t))dW t,(1953)
linking deterministic chaos with stochastic climate variability.
Noise-induced spectral deformation further modifies stability thresholds:
Re(λeff) = Re(λ) +δ noise(λ),(1954)
implying that stochastic forcing can either stabilize or destabilize near-critical regimes.
29.6 Implications for Predictability Horizons

The Universal Pellis Function Theory thus occupies a unifying position between nonlinear dynamics, statistical

30 Conclusions
The Universal Pellis Function Theory of Climate Criticality establishes a unified mathematical framework in which
climate dynamics are reformulated as the spectral evolution of a nonlinear operatorPacting on a high-dimensional
functional state space. In this formulation, the Earth system is no longer interpreted as a trajectory in phase space,
but as a continuously deforming spectral object whose stability is encoded in the geometry, density, and transport
properties ofσ(P). This reframing places climate science within a rigorous operator-theoretic paradigm connecting
dynamical systems, functional analysis, and statistical physics [1,2,4,6,18,23,33,37,139].
30.1 Summary of the Operator-Theoretic Framework
At the core of the theory is the nonlinear evolution equation:
d
dtX(t) =P(X(t)),(1962)
which generalizes classical dynamical systems by embedding multiscale feedbacks, renormalization effects, and non-
local interactions into a single operator-valued generator.
The emergent state admits a spectral representation:
X(t) =
Z
σ(P)
a(λ,t)ϕλdµ(λ),(1963)
where the amplitudesa(λ,t) evolve according to:
∂a(λ,t)
∂t =λa(λ,t) +N λ[a],(1964)
withN λ encoding nonlinear spectral coupling between modes.
The macroscopic climate state is thus interpreted as a measure-valued projection of a continuously evolving
spectral field:
µt(λ) =|a(λ,t)| 2dµ(λ).(1965)
30.2 Climate Criticality as Spectral Instability
A central result is that climate tipping points correspond to spectral crossings of the operatorP:
Re(λ) = 0,(1966)
which defines a codimension-one critical manifold separating dissipative and amplifying regimes.
The global instability functional is given by:
I[X] =
Z
σ(P)
Re(λ)|a(λ,t)| 2dµ(λ),(1967)
which acts as a Lyapunov-type diagnostic for global stability loss.
Near criticality, spectral condensation occurs:
µt(λ)⇀δ(λ−λ c),(1968)
indicating collapse of distributed modes into near-neutral eigenstructures.
30.3 Universality and Renormalization Structure
The renormalization invariance condition:
RP=γP(1969)
implies that climate dynamics belong to universality classes characterized by invariant spectral scaling laws.
The associated renormalization flow is: dP
ds =F(P),(1970)
with fixed points satisfying:
P∗ =RP∗.(1971)
Linearization around fixed points yields stability governed by spectral radius:
ρ(DR(P∗))<1,(1972)
defining attractor-like universality classes of climate behavior.

30.5 Interpretation of Planetary Instability
Planetary instability emerges as a global reorganization of spectral support:
σ(P)−→σ ′(P),(1977)
where instability is driven by redistribution of eigenvalue density toward the critical manifold.
In coupled Earth system representations, the operator takes block form:

with instability emerging when coupling amplification induces spectral alignment:

This mechanism provides a mathematical explanation for cascading transitions across subsystems, where
multiple tipping elements synchronize via shared spectral drift.
30.6 Final Synthesis
The theory culminates in the fundamental identification:
Climate Stability≡Spectral Stability ofP (1980)
Under this principle, tipping points, regime shifts, and cascading transitions are not exceptional anomalies,
but structurally inevitable consequences of nonlinear spectral deformation in high-dimensional operator fields.
Thus, the Universal Pellis Function Theory provides a mathematically coherent and scalable framework in
which bifurcation theory, renormalization group methods, and operator spectral analysis are unified into a single
predictive paradigm for planetary-scale criticality [4,6,11,18,23,33,37,139].
